IRISH AFFAIRS. NOTES FOR IRISH READERS.

CARSON'S BLLTI.F AND BLUSTER.

The great Orange Anti-Home Rule meet- I ing demonstration has come and gone. It must be confessed that a "declaration of faith," c:nstructed with lawyer-like discre- tion, from Sir Edward Carson seems, to the ordinary observer, to be a very flimsy foundation on which to base a "death struggle" against the decrees of the elective assembly which we call the Imperial Parlia- ment. But it is the best that the Irish "Die Hard," apparently were capable of in the very inauspicious circumstances in which they find themselves owing to the copious emissions of blatherskite in which it has been their custom to indulge. Of all the hollow pretences they are attempting to set up, the claim that the country is not to be consulted on Home Rule is, on every ground, the least convincing. For is it not patent to any man with the least facility for political memory and consistency that the country was consulted on Home Rule at the last two elections in the most direct and specific manner? The Unionists themselves saw to that. Mr. Balfour, Lord Lansdowne, Mr. Austen Chamberlain, Sir Edward Carson—the preacher of high treason—and his Falstaff's regiment, took the surest possible means of eliciting the opinion of the country on Home Rule by making Home Rule the first and most con- clusive reason why the Veto Bill should be rejected. Besides does any sane man imagine that if there were an appeal to the country to-morrow on Home Rule and if the country once more pronounced in its favour, no matter how unequivocally, that Sir Edward Carson and his treason advocates would accept the judgment? When the Veto Bill was adopted by the country, twice in succession with the fullest knowledge that its passage meant the passing of Home Rule, did these gentlemen accept the verdict of the country on the Veto Bill? The demand for another appeal to the country is as dishonest as the claim that the Protestants of Ireland would be discriminated against under Home Rule, and those who make it, know it full well.

TORIES READY TO COME TO TERMS. Consider the further point of the readiness of the Tory leaders, at a comparatively recent date to come to terms on the Home Rule question, which readiness the high priest of rebellion, Sir Edward Carson disputes. Th 3 autumn of 1910 is not far removed from present-day affairs and at a Conservative dinner held in Wimbledon on November Ist, 1910 this is what Mr. Chaplin said : " If anyone could devise a remedy to put the so-called Dominant Partner in a more equitable position, he would not decline to consider it." The Tory Press of that time, obviously expecting a " deal " at the Con- ference on Home Rule grew wonderfully enthusiastic in support of Home Rule "The Daily Express, "arch-enemy of Irish demands earlier on, adopted the assuring headline " The New Home Rule" and openly wrote of a settlement which had as its main feature readjustment of Ireland's relations with England and lastly we may cite the considered words of the most powerful writer the present Unionist party possesses, when the seer of the " Observer " Mr. J. L. Garvin wrote" From Mr. Redmond's programme of poperical Home Rule every suggestion of separation is explicitly eliminated. If Mr. Redmond is prepared to repeat and develop these declarations when he returns to this country and afterwards to stand by them. . . . the Unionist party in our conviction will be morally bound to confer with him." If Sir Edward Carson, indulging one moment in effusive expressions of loyalty to the Throne, and in the next unfolding a scheme idly threatening to subvert the Government on which that Throne rests will cease from his foolish display of what really constitutes treason-felony, if it were seriously regarded, and consult the plain facts of the case he will surely pause before he makes himself a laughing stock in his proposed march on Cork from Belfast. SIR A. CONAN DOYLE RECANTS.

From Mr. T. W. Russell, the man who did more than any other ten men to defeat Home Rule in the eighties, Sir Edward Carson is receiving daily refutations which must be disquieting to the Orange Party. But pro- bably an even more disconcerting document is the letter on Home Rule which a Belfast Tory paper in an evil moment drew from Sir Arthur Conan Doyle—another former 1 opponent to Home Rule and now a complete supporter of it. Sir Arthur Conan Doyle found he had to sink a good many of his old Liberal principles when he became a Unionist. He fought , two elections determinedly as a Unionist. I He wtN a most valued speaker or Unionist platforms and he has now been converted to, Home Rule by the conviction that conditions, existing been England and Ireland have undergone a change which renders the grant- ing of Home Rule to Ireland not only perfectly safe but a in Ater of high Imrerial concern. Like a good many other Unionists, Sir Arthur, who had an exceptional personal knowledge of the racial conditions in South Africa before

and during the war, has been deeply impressed by the healing effect which Home Rule had on feuds there which but a few years ago appeared to be absolutely incurable. When Lord Norton, a Tory peer, the other day declared his open mind on the Rome Rule question he was speaking for thousands of Englishmen, who, fierce opponents in the warring times cf the eighties, are now approaching our position along the eminently rational lines explained by Sir Arthur Conan Doyle and it makes more certain than ever that Sir Edward Carson's attempt to bluff the English people by the bluster and noise of Orange place-hunters is fore-doomed to ailure.

TLTESI)ArS SALE.

DEATH OF MR. JOHN GEDDES, J.P.

Another venerable and reepeted figure has been removed from our taidat by the death of Ur. John Geddes, J.P., head of the firm of John Geddes and Son, Ltd., Warrington, who passed away at Clifford Villa, Waverton, near Chester. on Saturday afternoon. Although he had attained the ripe old age of SI years, Mr. Geddes had until the last few m onths, enj ,yed remarkably good health. He resided at Et, Bold- street, Warringtoa, during the winter and at Waverton in the summer, and the astral change of residence took place in June last. S 3 )71 afterwards Mr. Geddes suffered a serious breakdown in health and was unable to travel to Warrington again. Notwithstanding the fact that he had long since passed the allotted span the news of his critical illness came as a am-prise to many people. for Mr. Geddee lived an active and useful life and every day he might ins seen walking to or from his nffi mt. His erectness and dignity of bear- ing and his benevolent, patriarchal countenance made him a @Viking figure and, although of a modest and retiring disposition, he had always a kindly smile and cheery word for his many friends. He shunned anything in the way of publicity or ostenta- tion, but did much va'uable work in a quiet way. A geod businessman. be took the keenest personal interest in the o'd established firm with which his name was identified. Duty" was his watchword and, although he did not associ tte hinaselfwith a large number of enterprises or organizations, lie mild always be relied upon for faithful and untiring service in any matter to which be set his hand. He was a great lover of home-life, a man of sterling character— to know intimately was a privilege—and his death creates a gap which it wil be difficult to fill. Mr. Geddes' business life had been chiefly devoted ta the firm of which he was the held—John Geddes and Son, Ltd., tea and coffee merchants. The busi- ness, which has a high reputation far beyond the confines of Warrington, was founded as far back as the year 1816, is Golden Square. It flourished so satisfactorily that twenty-seven yeais ego last November the present commodious premises in Peter- street were acquired. Mr. Geddes was also Chairman of the Longford Wire, Iron and Steel Company, Limited, a former director of the old Warrington %%rater Works Company, vice-chairman and a trustee of the Savings Bank in Buttermarket-street, and one of the commissioners of income, property and land taxes for the Warrington Division of Lincashire. He was aypointed a magistrate by the Chancellor of the Duchy of Lancaster in the year ISOI and discharged the duties most conscientiously, setting a good example of regular attendance at the Police Court. He was astannch Churchman and was probably the oldest member of the congregation of St. Paul's Church, to which he was much attached. In politics he was a Conservative but he never took part in electoral warfare.

PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

Another excellent programme is to be witnessed at this popular place of entertainment this week. " The 13evil's Elbow "is the title of a stirring little drama from the tarn of Mr. John F. Traynor, a popular author. The scene depicts a Canadian farm in the grip of a Canadian winter where the young wife of a settler is pining for the gaiety of London—a great contrast to her present life. Although her husband, whose Inv. for the wilds is untold, tries to console his young wife by the promise of an early visit to England, he is unsucceolul. To break the monotony he invites a neighbour to spend an evening with them, i man in whom he has great confidence and respect. In attending to the visitor's comfort occasion arises for him to leave the room to his wife and friend for • short while, during which time the two plan to decamp that night in an endeavour to try to get to England and arranging t 3 meet at the Deril's Elbow at 11 p.m. Aft, the visitor's departure the wife retires to rest on a Id a of ! sickness and the unsusracting husband it left 'to enjoy his pipe and make his nightly round. Shots signalling for help are later heard coming from the Devil's Elbow and the farmhands are despatched t-. the scene. The wife is carried into the house with the visitor and upon the husband's recognition of her he tffers the visitor a revolver and the duel that is fought ends fatally for the visitcr. Upon the husband's return the wife asks fur her verdict and in reply is told that when the spring appears she can j. urney to Londen but ton further ansser as to what will become of himself replies "God knows." The author and Miss Violet Alwyne as Harry and Myra Ellerton (husband and wife) play their parts to perfection while Mr. A. J. Raehort as Ralph Foster, by his cunning and sham loyalty, adds considerably to the success of the piece. Walter Grahame, the human marionette, earns warm approval for t e portrayal of a number of music hall stars from 1+475 to the present year. By the presentment rf A Eta Be's l'icture Post Card Album a most unique item is witnessed, some of the settings of which are pretty in the extreme. The Hanks create a good deal of genuine laughter in the scens entitled " Hampered." An original quick change character musician is seen in the person of Flu rnettie whose ability in handling, numerous instruments enables nun to execute selec- tions from the well-known composers in a most creditable manner. Stuart Bros. are eccentric ! acrobats of no mean order whilst 'Puree, the Australian !Bushman, contributes a musical turn which gives , complete satisfaction.

BIG ATTRACTION NEXT WEEK.

' The Gay Cordons.'

Seymour Hick,' production of" The 6ay Gordon..' will be stsged at the Palace and Hippodrome next week, the performacce taking up the whole of the evening. The play opens with dawn breaking on a moor in the Highlands ; presently figures in Highland costume give life to the scene. Then the shooting patty from Meltrose Castle appear, and among them is pretty Petty Quainton, the heiress of the minim: airo American, Andrew Quainton, who, for the time being, is the owner of the shooting and the occupier of the Castle. Peggy has original thoughts on the subject of marriage. Her wealth has tempted many suitors. and she has rejected them all ; she wants to be loved for herself, by some honest, handsome and brave man, who shall know nothing of her position and prospects in life. In a moment of wild inspiration and girlish irresp-msibility, she changes places with Victoria Siddons, the daughter of the proprietor of a peripatetic Punch and Judy Show, aid who acts as " help " to Widow Janet McCleod, the tenant of the cottage on the left. The widow is in a state of suppressed excitement, for she is expecting her foster-son, Angus Graeme. a private in the famous Highland reg:ment, the Gay Gordans. Presently Angus, in tartan and kilt, make,. his appearance, aid the first person be meets is the , heiress iii her masquerading attire. Like the gallant I soldier Le is, the pretty face of the maiden makes an instantaneous impres.ion, and before either of them are well aware of their words, they find themselves. deeply engaged in a sentimental conversation. Peggy is delighted will the young soldier, and plays her part of " help " to perfecti. n. T 1 e remainder of the story is equally fascinating, and should be seen by all. ROME.

THE ARCH OF TITUS.

By Charles E. Parker.

In the spring of this year I visited Italy *together with my wife and daughter. We naturally spent a good deal of our time in Rome. The interests in this wonderful city, which for several centuries merited the title .of the "Mistress of the World," are so 'many that any comprehensive description is .beyond my powers. A description and an illustration of one of the Arches may how- ever be of interest to the readers of the Examiner." The arch I speak of is the .marble Arch of Titus. It stands at the high North end of the Roman Forum. The old Roman road the "Via Sacra" winds through -the Forum from the North end, rises to the -summit of the high ground, at the South, passes through the Arch of Titus and descends to the Coliseum about five hundred yards away, which gigantic structure stands about the same level as the lower or Northern end of the Forum. The Arch of Titus was built in 82 A.D. to commemorate the seige of Jerusalem. On this Arch are the bas-reliefs representing the spoils, the gold and silver vessels taken from the Temple at Jerusalem. As this Arch represents the overthrow of the Holy City no orthodox Jew ever passes under it ;he

[ OF TITUS C. E. Parker).

So now %was seen of Appian. When stones began to fly, He shook, and crouched, and wrung his hands, and smote upon his thigh, 'Kind client., honest lictors, stand by me in this fray ! Must Ibe torn in pieces ? Home, home, the nearest way!' While yet he spoke, and looked around with a bewildered stare, Four sturdy lictors put their necks beneath the cantle chair; And fourscore clients on the left, and fourscore on the right, Arrayed themselves with swords and staves, and loins gilt up for fight. But, though without or staff or sword, t 33 furious was the throng, That scarce the train with might and main could bring their lord along. Twelve times the crowd made at him; five times they seized his gown; Small chance was his to rise again, if once they got him down And sharper came the pelting; and evermore the yell— 'Tribunes! we will have Tribunes ! '—rose with a louder swell ; And the chair tossed as tosses a bark with tattered sail When raves the Adriatic beneath an es.tern gale. One atone Lit Appian in the mouth, and one beneath the err; And ere he reached Mount Palatine, he swocried with p in and fear. His cursed head, that he was wont to hold so high with pride, Now like a drunken mates, hung down, and swayed from side to bide ; And when his stout retainers had brought him to his door, His face and neck were all one cake of filth and clotted gore. As Appius Claudius was that day, so may his grandson be ! God send Rome one such other sight, and send me there to see. This prancing poetry is Lord Macaulay's but the main facts are historical. But in my excursion into the past, wherein I had travelled not miles, but ages. I had almost forgotten my sketch, and when I observed my arch I found it looked quite different, neither sun nor clouds had stood still on Palatine, whatever they did on Gilboa. All the shadows were altered, both in direction and depth, so I tore up my first effort and began again and on the strength of the nuts and oranges finished the sketch. I ' was so pleased with myself, my nuts, my oranges and sketch that I determined I would offer the drawing to the" Examiner" and lam told it is accepted. This is my first published effort as an artist and I feel as proud as a youth when he has his first shave.

THE LATEST IN CARPETS.

From thea.cle—sig",;.—oi.their —OW artists, Messrs. Goodells, of King-street, Manchester, have entered into arrangements with a British firm of manufacturers to make for them exclusively a speciality carpet which is oertsin to meet with much favour. The "Allgood" carpet it is aptly styled. It is reversible, seamless, and durable, beautiful in colour and in design, and at the same time inexpensive. Messrs. Goodalls' csrpet show- rooms are stocked with the finest carpets that the world produces, and In their specialisation the firs have scored many success s. By the "Allgood" they are prepared to be judged. ===== 0002601_19110930_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ARCH (drawn by Mr.

comes up the" Via Sacra" and turns to the right or left and makes his way through broken columns and rejoins the road beyond the Arch. I could not procure a satisfac- tory photograph of this famous Arch so deter- mined to make an attempt to draw it. I often wonder why holiday makers and travellers all do not use their pencils more and sketch the things they see. Is it not true that we -never see a thing accurately until we can reproduce it? I do not mean that there is not an accurate photograph upon the retina of the eye, but this photograph is not fully conveyed to the mind. Very few of us can ever aspire to be artists, but many might with a little industry be able to draw sufficiently well to get a permanent representation of what we see, which in after years would be a source of interest to ourselves and others. It is only when you begin to put on paper what you see, or think you see, that you realise how inaccurate is your conception of an object. You find that your ideas of form, of light and shade, are altogether at fault. Now in my own case, while I can never be an artist, with practice, mostly in odd moments which otherwise might be wasted, I have attained sufficient proficiency to draw one of those animals that gives milk,—and need not now, for purpose of identification, so that all the world may know, write beneath "This is a But to the Arch. I sallied forth one bright morning when the sun,the oldest and greatest of artists, was throwing into bold relief the high lights and necessarily into deep black- ness, the shadows. Some might think that it would be a very much easier thing to have taken a Kodak and, by the help of this same sun, got a very much more accurate repre- sentation by photography than could be got by an indifferent draughtsman with paper and pencil. But in so doing I should have lost much of the interest, and not known, as now I do, this simple but perfect piece of architec- ture, which for two thousand years has -stood a thing of beauty, and therefore for- .ever a joy. First I had to chose my stand, and selected a position outside the Forum looking westward, and having the Palatine hill as background for the Arch. I had a great deal of trouble in getting my drawing into correct perspective, because, for me at least, I was taking it at an awkward angle, and so had to use rubber far more than I liked. My arch seemed drunk, it would not have stood two hundred years far less two thousand. I was beginning to get desperate when I spied a cheery old woman selling nuts and oranges. We soon became friends and she got some of my centesimi and I some of her nuts and oranges. Nuts and oranges make a good lunch: at any rate in Rome. We noticed many Roman workmen made this midday meal of oranges and dry bread; at times they had bread and sardines. Butter is little used among the working people, the price of this as of many other things within the reach of our workers, is prohibitive. Well, I enjoyed my oranges and nuts ; the nuts I cracked with one of the stones of the Forum and then over a pipe—all artists smoke pipes—I let my fancy free. Here was this stone which I had used as a nut-cracker. If this broken piece of marble had brains and memory, and tongue, what tales it might tell. Was it a piece chiselled off in the cutting of the capitol of some stately column for the temple of a god, or a chip from a rough block in which a famous sculptor discerned a sleeping Venus? Did this piece of marble witness those scenes in the Forum which went to shape the history of the race? Did it see those great assemblies of Roman freemen gathered together to exercise their power and to decide the fate of kings and nations? Was it a spectator in those many scenes of civic conflict which, from their frequency and sanguinary character, make us think that every inch of the Forum must at one time or other have been stained with blood? Was it present at that :memorable scene made familiar to us by Shakespeare when to the Forum they brought the body of Julius Carsar, the invader of Britain nd the greatest Roman of them al,.

brought after his assassination by Brutus and his fellow conspirators at the base of Pompey's statue? Was this chunk of marble there when from the Rostrum in the Forum, by leave of Brutus, Mark Antony delivered his famous oration with the mantle-covered corpse of Czesar at his feet, which oration so roused the Roman populace that Brutus aad his fellow conspirators had to flee the city to escape their wrath. The English words of the oration are Shakes- peare's, the oration itself and its effect on the populace are history. And was this stone handy when Virginia in the Forum slew his little daughter to save her from slavery and shame, and was it one of the stones hurled at Appius Claudius when the roused populace battered him nearly to death ? '

NOTES BY " OVALIST."

Warrington is to a have County match—Lancashire v. Cumberland---a week on Monday. The new stand and ground improvements have thus brought early recognition from the authorities. I hope members will feel gratified by the honour which the County Union bas done the Warrington Club, and ,take in Sporting spirit the regulation which obliges them to pay to see the match.

There are many interesting topics of conversation in football circles to-day, and one of the most absorb- ing is the Frawley case. The Union had a meeting to discuss the matter on Tuesday, but no settlement was come to and a further meeting will be held on Tuesday next. Warrington are holding firmly to their rights in the matter, and I think the Union will have no option but to decide in favour of the Wirepullers. In that case Frawley will doubtless turn out for Warrington. The yarn which Manchester papers persist in publishing to the effect that Warrington are considering the transfer of Frawley to Hull is all bunkum. It would be as much as the Committee's lives are worth to do anything of the kind.

Frawley's reception on Saturday when he turned up unexpectedly at the'A" team match was a revelation of his popularity with the Warrington crowd, and had a marked effect on the Australian. If be had any doubt of his welcome, he has none .now, and in conversation expreseed his willingness to don the" primrose and blue" once more if the Union decided that he was bound to the Warrington club. Frawley looks exceedingly" fit" and well, and I hope to see him doing himself full justice in the Warring- ton ranks before many more matches have gone by. Two seasons ago he was unfortunate in the matter of injuries,and uever gave more than ozcasional glimpses of the real Frawley. Since then he has increased his reputation" down under."

Stunts, he tells us, is playing a game in inter-state matches, but has put on weight O'Malley has retired from the game, and is devoting himself to business in Sydney.

At,d now a word about Papakura, before we turn from individuals to matches. The Maori turned out with the " A "team at Wilderspool on Saturday, but he would have been better advi,ed had he postponed his debut until he had g 4 iuto something like football fitnees. Ile carried quite a s one c.f superthous flesh , and was consequently very st,tv in the centre. What !little he bad to do he did in a manner which showed that he could play football, tut be will need to perform a modern miracle to get back to first-claes form. Frawley says that " Papa " at his best was the finest fullback he had ever seen. That " best "was only two years ago. So there is room for hope. Would it not be a good plan ti let Papakura tun (Algoma weight la the forwards?

The Warrington men did quite as well as expected last week-end. The win at Barrow was an improve- ment on last season's result, and was earned by good all-raund football. Brooks, Creever, Taylor and Thomas all shone on the attack, and Bradshaw got the best try he has scored for Warringtoa f o a long time. His display was altogether better, b2ing marked by more emfidence and da:h.

At Huddersfield on Monday, the Wirepuller') met their masters. Before the match I wagered a cigar that Hudderrsfield would win by 20 pints, ad when the final score read 21-0, I felt somewhat of a modern prophet. Though steady rain spoiled the game somewhat, the display was full of interest, parti- cularly Iron) a Huddersfield point of view. Warrington rarely did anything t) flutter even a partisan enthusiast's pulse, and if they bad been playing till now I don't think they would have scored a try. The secret of the home team's easy triumph was pace and cleverness. The Warrington threequarters wn.e out of the hunt entirely, though the forwards played a [grand game in the scrumnyige.

Brooks and Creevey burst through more than once but there was no support behind. In fact, on one occasion, there was the remarkable sight of Creevey in the home quarter beckoning to Clare and Taylor to come up and take the pass. With a couple of speedy men behind there was a gift of a try under the pans. As it was the half back's clever break-away was C3M- pletely wasted. I ought to add that, well as Brooke p'ayed most of the time, he had a mad fit at the beginning and twice threw the ball recklessly into the bands of he Huddersfield skipper, Wagstaff, and 8 points debit was the result in the first quarter of an hour. Tilley distinguished himself with a defensive disp'ay which was cheered all round-the ground. lie kept the home team's score down considerably. But what Warrington lacks is a three-quarter line of speedier men, and particularly at centre.

I am glad that for to-day's match with Swinton young Carter is being given a trial. Wallington are a bit inclined to keep promising juniors too long in the " A " team, when they would probably improve quicker in better company. If a lad has talent in him ptactice with better players should bring it out. As an instance, two of Huddersfield's crack three- quarters —Wagstaff and Ifoorhouse—are only 20 years old, and they have both been playing senior football for ' quite two years. These two are, no doubt, exoeptional but they might never have developed if they had been confined to an "A " team. Competition and respon- sibility bring out the belt in a man.

The team for to-day will be : Tilley, full baok ; Woodburn, Carter, Taylor and Bradshaw, three- quarters ; Brooks and Creevey, half backs ; Johnston. Shugars, Thomas, Cheaters, Brocklehurst and Arnold, I forwards. The last-named played a capital game last Saturday, scrummaging and handling the ball equally well, and he deserves his promotion. The man whom something more is expected of just now is the skipper!

Two alterations ara announced in the Sminton team, Evans displacing Williams at centre three- quarter and Preston taking the place of ll'Veigh in the forwards. The team is as IJllows

Johnson ; Parker, Valentine. Evans, Mrrio; Davies (1)10, Wharton; Blears, Bailey, Davies (0. B)., Garirell, Prestm, Randall.

A Win at Barrow.

0 0 0 2 3 13

• Saturday's match at Barrow afforded Warrington no opportunity of gaining their first victory of the season on foreign soil. Both teams were as selected, and a capital crowd assembled for what is always considered I a popular fixture on the Furness's Club's list. Unfortu- nately the weather turned out wet and militated against an exposition of the finer points of play by bath teams. The officials and players were: Warrington.—Tilley; Bradshaw, Taylor, Unsworth Woodburn •, Brookes, Creevey ; Saugars, Joht.ston, Cheaters, Naylor, Brocklehurat, aid Thomaa. Barrow.— Morgan; Bomber, Dolman, Treloat, Gifford; Mitchell, Fallowfield ; Atterbury, Youldoit, Logan, Mason, MoLester, and Brown. Referee : Mr. P. H. Jackson, St. Helens ; touch judges: Mr. W. Hall, Holme ; and Mr. C. Abrahams, Kendal. Barrow lost the toss and startid against a strong breeze, Tilley early putting in a huge punt which went deal. The home lot showed plenty of energy, and by dashing play forced Warrington to defend. Tilley's clearance being especially fine Brooks, Johnston and and Naylor lead the way to Barrow quarters, and there was some good handling of the ball which broke down at Woodburn. A kick by Treloar was charged down, and Morgan was lucky to save from the Warrington forwards. Creevey and Brooks made fine efforts to cross, but the home tanks tackled in desperate fashion. Thomas failed at a penalty goal, end a cross-kick by Bradshaw in front of the posts also failed to materialise. A flue attack by Barrow was only just checkmated. Gifford being beaten for the touch by inches. Dobson and Creevey both got in the wars and had to retire. With play at midfield Bradshav secured, and dodging the borne wing cleverly he raced away and completed a Sue try behind the posts, for Woodburn to kick a goal. At half-time, Warrington led by 5 points to nil. On resuming, Barrow were abort of Dolasan, but Warrington were at full strength again. Very soon Johnston, Thomas and Shugars led a raid on the Barrow line, which had remarkable escapes. Taylor and Brad- shaw combined well and the wing man essayed to kick past Morgan, but in attempting to reach tha oval his progress was barred. Warrington again passed finely, but found the tackling lacking nothing in its keenness. Eventual:y Barrow had a spell on the aggressive end though Tilley was forced over his line he showed all his brilliant resource and a huge kick when hard pressed removed danger to centre field. As the game waged on Birrow shined signs of falling away and pressure was applied by the Warrington forwards. A scare always seemed imminent and the visitors gained. Brookes served Creevey for that player to dodge through the defence and score. There was no goal. The home team did mak3 a few 'spasmodic rushes near the close, but they could not overcome Tilley. Near the end Wood- burn gut away, but hesitated at Morgan, who tickled finely. Result :—Warrington, one goal two tries (eight points), Barrow nil.

Bowling Notes. WARRINGTON WORKS v. CADEDONIANS. Sportmen generally are nothing if not charitable and no doubt it was this motive which impelled the teams of the Watrington Works Association and the - Caledonions to play the bowling match arranged between them last Saturday, is spite of the almost incessant rain. The proceeds were in aid of the Nursing Aasoc'ation and the Poor Children's Summer Camp, and it is anticipated that these two organisations will greatly profit through the match.

It was decided that those who wished should play games of 15 up instead of 21 so that they should not be erposed to the weather for a lumer time. This was greatly appreciated and when the bowling com- menced the players set to with a will, with the result that some keen competitions erased.

Alderman Forshaw was matched against T. Hackett. The game was a neck and neck lace, though the Alderman bad a slight advantage all the way up to the 18th end when he was rased. the scoring card recording, 12-11. Bat he Made a fine rally and with. the aid of two couples ran home victor by 3 points.

One of the best games was that between George Ball (Works As,ocistion) and W. Murray. They peeled at 11 aud then the former getting a nice mark ran up to 15 while his opponent could only add a unit. However, Murray made a grand attempt and after egual6ing at 17 gained an advantage of 2. A splendid tessel was won by Ball, by the ruargia of 1 point.

Councillor T. Steel (Caledonians) vanquished S. Allen by 7 points in a game of 21 up. He obtained Pt chalks while Allen only registered 7 and main- tained a substantial difference to the end.

Albert Gleave (Work.) had quite an easy ta4k .ith T. Cooper whom he defeated by 16 points. The latter had hardly a look in.

There is no under- GERMANY AND standing the German MR. Mc..KENNA. newspapers—in any other sense, that is to say, elan that they are bent on picking a quarrel with England. Their arrangement with their correspondents in London seems to be for the supply of information which has no foundation in fact. Greatly to find cause of quarrel in a straw is, according to one of Shakespeare's characters, an indication of the heroic spirit; but the German Press, which is to a large extent under Ministerial control, does not require even the straw as a provocation. The newspapers of Berlin have been unanimously falling foul of a speech by Mr. MCKENNA, our First Lord of the admiralty, who is accused of having spoken about Germany's methods of intimidation in Persia and elsewhere, and of making unfavourable comparisons between the British Foreign Office and that of Berlin. As a matter of fact, Mr. MCKENNA said nothing on either of these subjects. He made no reference to any Foreign Office whatever, and he said not a single word about Persia or German intimidation. The comments of the Berlin Press, therefore, are based on sheer invention. There is an object not very dimly discernible. The German Government wishes to raise more money for its navy, and the best means of reconciling the people to additional taxation is adjudged to be the getting up of an anti-British scare. We are sorry to be driven to admit that, though there was nothing in Mr. MCKENNA'S speech which justifies the German com- ments, he cannot be held entirely blameless in the matter. The German Press, and, we may be sure, the German Government, does not forget that the FIRST LORD OF THE ADMIRALTY occasioned a Navy scare in this country on the basis of misinformation as to the speed at which the German warships were being built. There is no doubt, of course, that Mr. MCKENNA was misled on that occasion. A British

Minister who deliberately misled the country on matters of such crucial importance would be driven from office as soon as he was found out. The First Lord believed what he had been told, and induced the Government to act upon his information but the subject was one on which he ought to have been well-informed, and the German Press, ruthlessly pursuing its purpose, declines to admit that he acted under a false. impression. It says, and possibly believes, that he consciously and designedly misled the British Parliament, and in this way justifies such an acceleration of speed in German shipbuilding as was said, without foundation, to have taken place at an earlier date.

The present amazing misrepresentation of the First Lord shows how injudicious gild costly it is to build ships in open and declared competition with another Power. Ship- building should be bised upon the necessities of the situation. The prime necessity is that Great Britain should retain command of the sea, This is admitted even by Germany. We are compelled to base our naval pro- gramme on this understanding. It has been declared by the PRIME MINISTER that the necessary naval strength is 10 per cent. margin over the next three naval Powers o Europe. Whilst maintaining the standar d is it necessary to speak continually as is done by men of both parties, as if we were preparing for a naval war with Germany? Surely not. The consequence is an accelerated and most burdensome competi- tion. We obtain no real advantage, how- ever swiftly we build, because Germany continues to build against us. There is no good reason why it ihould do so. We are not likely wantonly to attack German interests in any part of the world. This fact is tacitly admitted by the frequently-repeated declaration that German ship-building is not in competition with Great Britain; but a necessary provision for the protection of an increasing German commerce. This pretence, we are afraid, is only partially sincere, and the present attack on Mr. MCKENNA, which is really an attack on British naval policy exposes it in its true character.

The trumpeted inten- THE PROGRESS tions of that "ascend- O" ULSTERIA." ency " Party which has adopted Sir Edward Carson as its leader on the ground that he is the one man with a "large brain" that Orangeism has produced, give away the Ulster case in the most ruthless fashion. Here is a minority in Ireland which declares that it will not loyally accept the decision of the Imperial Parliament, because that decision would set u? the natural balance between a majority and a minority. Where the loyalty comes in no one but a devotee of the strange creed of Tariff Reform-cum- Orangeism- cum - Landlordism could possibly imagine. If a minority which cannot get its own way in everything is justi- fied in challenging the Imperial Government to come on, at its peril, and carry into effect the legislation of the Imperial Parliament, and claims for itself the title of " loyalists " because it defies the Imperial Parliament, the grievances of the great majority of a nation, overridden for centuries by the selfish interests of an " ascendency " clique, are proved up to the hilt. The section of Ulster Orangeism which Sir Edward Carson invites to palpable revolt takes its stand upon this extraordinary paradox—it is only " loyal " when loyalty " serves its own ends.

Mr. Harold Smith still THAT WIRE remains disappointingly TANGLE! silent in regard to his famous proposition about purchasing wire made in Warrington, notwithstanding the challenges to argue the point from his constituents, Messrs. Pierce Williams and Osborne. He is a wise man who knows when to say " nuffin," but the electors will soon become possessed of the idea that the member's bravery is reserved for exhibition only on the party platform. By the way, to take Mr. Smith's proposition up once more, if the Corporation ordered a quantity of wire from Messrs. Rylands Bros., which they could only supply at £3,000, but which could be purchased in tlermany at £2,750 would the order, really benefit Warrington workingmen by the direct employment of labour? We should like Mr. Smith or Mr. Rylands to answer the question, because we have a shrewd suspicion that Messrs Rylands Bros. would, under the circumstances, buy the wire from Germany and make a safe and easy profit as middlemen. There would be no need for wire-drawing in Warrington unless the Britisher could beat the German in the home market as he does every day in the week. What a wiry tangle Mr. Smith has got himself in!

There is reasonable POLITICS AND doubt as to whether the BUSINE.9S AT directors of the Pearson P. & K. and Knowles Coal and

Iron Company Ltd. are interested as much in business as in politics. Certainly there is as much of the latter as of the former at the annual meetings of the Company, and it may be that profits are so easily made that the chief spokesmen of the concern feel bound to find something extraneous to complain about. The financial year just closed, for instance, has left the company- with a net profit of .£70,977, exclusive of the balance of £15,968 from the previous year. All the year's additions, £19,500, are written off, and £15,000 is added to the reserve fund out of profits, while the preference shareholders get their six percent. and the ordinary shareholders four per cent. for the year. No wonder, the Chairman, Mr. Harmood Banner, expresses satisfaction with the annual report, and we can only conclude that he and the Messrs. Bleckly are anxious for something or somebody to quarrel with when they complain of the burdens of the Coal Mines Bill, the Eight Hours Bill, the prospective charges of National Insurance and refer to the Chancellor's " plundering and blundering." It is a source of joy to us that so great an industrial concern as "P. and K," playing so large a part in the life of Warring- ton, can pursue the even and profitable tenour of its way despite the impositions of a " predatory " Government. There must be some wisdom embodied in the advanced legislation of to-day or instead of merely exercising the proud privilege of grumbling, with success at their backs, the directors of our great manufacturing firms would be pointing to financial disasters and irretrievable loss. For the first time for some years Messrs. Harmood Banner and Bleckly have had nothing to say about Tariff Reform. Only a few short years ago it was put forward resolutely as the one salvation of the manufacturer. Each annual meeting of this company saw its need emphasized more and more, until we almost feared that without the great panacea, the doors at Bewsey would have to be closed. Somehow, industry and commercial acumen have triumphed once again ; but why is the cry for Tariff Reform quiet and stilled? Evidently because the country has grown tired of its hollow echoings, and because even the Conservative Party recognises that a new policy of real reform at home is the only thing that can rehabilitate it in the eyes of the level-headed English people.

Those who have A WORD FOR followed the proceed- THE VOTER. ings before the Revision Courts may be forgiven if they read with some surprise the accurate statement of the law made by the Revising Barrister at Clacton. "It was," he said, the duty of the overseers to see that the lists were presented in such a way as ensured persons ent:tled to the franchise being p'acei thereon, and a failure in that duty laid the overseers open to a fine. The business of obtaining a vote is intended in fact (says the "Morning Leader ") to be made as easy as possible for the duly qualified citizen; it is not meant to be the prize for victory in a spirited contention between experts financed by rival parties. It is a good thing to have the law clearly stated. The facts often give quite a different impression.

The by-election in the Two BREAKING Kilmarnock Burghs POINTS IN TORYISM. has given the Tory Party the opportunity of showing, once for all, whether they really wished, as they declared in the House of Commons,to co-operate with the Government in making the National Insurance Bill a measure which would do as much for this great scheme as present conditions will allow, or whether they would try to stir up ill-feeling against it for the narrowest partisan purposes. The Tory Party has met its test, and it has answered to it. They have tried their best to kill National Insurance, though they would like to come in at the end, if all the difficulties are surmounted, and claim credit for the reform, just as they did with Old Age Pensions. As for the Welsh ecclesiastics who are trying to stimulate resistance to a measure for Disestablishment, they should first settle their grievances with the Tariff Reform organs like the Pall Mall Gazette," which ask why the Tariff Reform Party should be expected—if it is allowable to sum up their position in Bishop Butler's historic phrase— "to prop a falling church " ?

SALE OF A AMOUS STUD. _ Walton Hackneys Realise 2,905 Guineas The famous stud or high-class pedigree hackneys and hackney ponies owned by Sir Gilbert Greenall, Burt., was disposed of by public auction at Walton Hall on Thursday. The opportunity thus afforded of obtaining some of the best-bred animals in England attracted a large attendance of breeders and buyers from all parts of the Kingdom, Owing to a re-arrangement of farms on his English estates, Sir Gilbert is giving op the Welton stud, but in Ireland, where he has bought an estate, he intends to go in for breeding thoroughbreds land horses likely to make bunters. It is expected that certain times bunters will be housed in Walton stables. The sale was conducted by Mr. W. Bainbridge. Bidding for many of the lots was brisk and, on the whole, good prices were realized. The total sum changing hands was 2,905 guineas, giving an average of £55 18s. At Padgate on Thursday Alderman Bennett said that Mr. C. E. Parker "was both a Wesleyan and a good citizen--a combina- tion which, he was sorry to say, did not always go together." We wonder if Mr. Parker will give Alderman Bennett the same testimonial

..... 0,0 " I consider the prospects are excellent for good trade during the autumn and winter, provided there is no strike among the colliers and railway men" writes Mr. Richard T. Fairclough, president of the Warrington Chamber of Commerce in reply to the " Daily Mail's,"request for opinions on the comme.cial outlook. *

The percentage of Conservatives in the total number of electors in Kilmarnock Boroughs has fallen as low as 27 in the elec- tion just concluded. In 1888 it was 35, and in 1900 it was 45. The figure just recorded is the lowest in the modern records of the constituency. Gladstone is still a name to to conjure with ! * * *

Mr. Keir-Hardie, M.P., will not feel flattered at the Kilmarnock result. His strenuous efforts to persuade the working- men that a vote for a Liberal meant a vote for shooting down strikers has recoiled on his own head. By the way, what phase of political thought does Mr. Keir-Hardie represent now-a-days? • *

According to the Cl;airman of the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron, Co., Ltd., all consumers, traders and householders would be wise to lay in a stock of coal sufficient to ticie them over the winter months. Of course, the miners may strike, but in any case the advice is excellent—especially from the point of view of the colliery owner. * * *

A notable entry on ;That all will hope will be a political career worthy of the great name he bears has been made by Mr. W. G. C. Gladstone, the grandson of W. E. Glad- stone. His achievement in holding the Kilmarnock Burghs for Liberalism by the magnificent majority of 2286 against both a Tory and a Labour Candidate would be remarkable in an experienced politician. Accomplished by one so young, it is full of promise for his future. • _

His Worship the Njayor has accepted an invitation to fill the office of chief magistrate for another year. We are sure the intima- tion will be welcome news to the towns people, for Councillor Dr. Joseph has made friends wherever he has gone in his corporate capacity, and has shown a determination during his year of office to sink all personal considerations for the good of the community, which is worthy of all praise.

The Bishop of London does not like the Mohammedan religion, but he freely recog- nises its good points : Thee were certain things which the Moslems did which he wished every Christian would do. One was to be absolutely open in one's witness. lie would like to order prayer mats for all the the Bishops to use at twelve o'c'ock in the

Athemeum before their fellow-members, and also for Christians to us a in factories and warehouses. We are content to put on record the sugges- tion, in the hope that some member of the Athena3um will be good enough to tell us if the Bishop's advice leads to any actual use of the mat. *53

The purveying of sweets does not necessarily guarantee a sweet temper. In, the local police court this week two sweet vendors in the Warrington Market have been charged with committing a breach of the peace. From the facts related it seems that the defendants rent adjoining stalls, and one having been underselling the other, a lively dispute arose which culminated in police intervention. Obviously the Markets Com- mittee has made a mistake in grouping tenants together according to their respective trades. Competition is naturally a little too close to be tolerable under the circumstances.

Mr. Harmood Banner, M.P., has discovered who are the true philanthropists. At the annual meeting of the Pearson and Knowles Co., Ltd., he demonstrated to his own satis- faction that the shareholders were the true philanthropists because they found facilities for the employment of 9,447 men. He clinched the argument by observing that the 9,447 employees drew £631,180 yearly in wages, while the 1,161 shareholders only got £39,086. What he failed to point out was that the employees earned all they got —and more in many cases—while the " philanthropists " sat at home and enjoyed the fruits of that labour. We are far from suggesting that capital is not entitled to its fair return, but we should never have ven- tured on such an original theory that the man who bought shares with a single eye to dividends was a philanthropist in the true meaning of the word.

The veteran leader of the Salvation Army has just completed another strenuous cam- paign. It was the seventh of the series that has marked his journeying to and fro in the United Kingdom, and it concluded on Mon- day at Cardiff. Starting from Leigh,General Booth travelled through Lancashire,Cheshire, Yorkshire, the Midlands, and South Wales, the distance covered being about 1,600 miles. For so old a man this itinerary was a note- worthy achievement, but in addition he addressed sixty meetings, sometimes con- ducting three on one day, besides speaking to groups of people at informal stoppages on the journey. The "General" expressed himself as especially grateful for the kind and hospitable receptions he experienced. The mayors of Corporate boroughs, the chairmen of local councils, and at times members of Parliament had taken part in his meetings and shown him many courtesies. Many kindnesses had also been shown the "General" by the clergy of the Anglican Church, and also by ministers of the Free Churches.

• • These are a few Dont's for public speakers which Mr. Grenville Kleiser has issued to the I members of public speaking clubs : Don't apologise. Don't shout. Don't hesi- tate. Don't attitudinise. Don't speak in a high key. Don't pace the platform. Don't distort your words. Don't exceed your time limit. Don't indulge in personalities. Don't emphasise everything. Don't praise your- self. Don't tell a long story. Don't sway your body. Don't be "funny." Don't fatigue your audience. Don't speak through , closed teeth. Don't! drink while speaking. Don't fumble with your clothes. Don't be sarcastic. .Don't "hem " and "haw." Don't stand like a statue. Don't clear your throat. Don't declaim. Don't speak rapidly. Don't antagonise. Don't fidget. Don't over-gesticu- late. Don't wander from your subject. Don't be awkward. Don't address the ceiling. Don't be monotonous. Don't put your hands on your hips. Don't be violent. Don't rise on your toes. Don't forget to sit down when you have finished. In brief :

Stand up so that you can be seen. Speak up so that you can be heard. Shut up so that you will be liked.

(Hear, hear.) The Rev. J. Grange Howlett devoted an impressive address to urging the personal responsibility of Sunday School teachers for the souls of their scholars. The Rev. H. A. Lester.

Presentation on his Departure for London. Os all hands there have been expressions of regret— and at the same time congratulation—at the departure of the Rev. H. A. Lester (vice-principal at the Warrington Training College, and curate at St. Thomas' Church, Stockton Heath) for London, where he is to occupy the responsible post of director of Sunday schools in that diocese. The rev, gentleman preached his farewell sermon at St. Thomas Church on Sunday f evening and evidence of the esteem entertained for him was afforded by the large congregation present. Mr. Lester hen this week been the recipient of several handsome presentations. On Tuesday evening at the c nclusion of the annual meeting of the Warrington and District Church House he was presented by the members with a handsome silver casket, a cheque and an address of best wishes for his future service to the Church. Mr. BERESFORD MELVILLE made the presentation and remarked that Mr. Lester was at the bottom of the movement to start the Church Home and had taken the keenest interest in its work. Archdeacon SPOONER raid that while they deeply regretted Mr. Lester's departure they rejoiced in his new appointment, which his epecital qualifications would enable him to fulfil efficiently in the service of the community and the whole Church of Chriat. (Applause). The Rev, Canon STEVENSON Principal of I.le Train- ing College) said that he had not worked for 13 yearn with Mr. Lester without feeling the coming parting very keenly indeed. One thing had struck him in reference to Mr. Lester, and that was his unbounded energy. They could not ratify him with work. Not only was the Training College indebted to him, but the whole of the town and neighbourhood for many things, including his services as secretary of the Church Society and in connection with missionary work. He had long felt that the time land arrived when Mr.

The Church House "The Home of Church Life.'

"-The annual meeting of the' Warrington and District Church House " wan held on Tuesday evening in the spacious room at the Church House, Bridge-street. Canon Willie presided over a good attendance. Among those present were: Archdiacon Spooner, Canon Stevenson, the clergy of the town and district, Mr. Robson .1. Chorley (hoc. secretary), and Mr. H. Beresford Melville (bon. treasurer). The accounts showed that the receipts for the year endin; March amounted to £69 18s 54:1 including sub- scriptions and donations ESO Is 61. The payments included:—T J Lee, ore year's rent 05, and Warrington Corporation rates, £l3 4s 4d. The Hon Treasurer said that the membership of the Church House bad not increased since it was opened two years ago and he regretted to say that unless there was a very great improvement in the financial support given, in another twelve months they would have a balance on the wrong side. If they could get more funds they would make many improvements, which wanld make the Church House more efficient. They deserved to be supported, as the House was of immense benefit to the work of the Church. Mr. H. WOODS moved that the balance sheet be adapted, and the Rev. JAMES COLLINS in seconding, suggested that a method of stimulating interest in the Church House would be to hold a " conversazione " in the Cc-eperative Hall, at which the members of the Church of England in the district would have the , matter placed before them. The accounts were adopted. Canon WILLIS said that the Church House was a visible sign sod proof of unity, as all the incumbents ,f Warrington now belonged to the Church House. "hey had got a respectable and handsome place in Thick they could spend some of their time to wdvantage, but he hoped that instead of merely having awo rooms they would have the whole of the rooms at tiberty in that building. 1 on the proposition of the Rev. JAMES COLLINS the following were re-elected :—The Rev Canon Willis (chairman), Mr. lt J Chorley (boo secretary), Mr. H B Melville (hen treasurer), committee, Revs W Bracecamp, G W Dickenaon, H H Corsble, Messrs Seger Owen, J H Smethurst, Oswald Lofthouse and T J Lee. Archdeacon SPOONER delivered an address on " A Church House, the need for it and its utility." He said that such an institution as that Church House was evidential of Church activity and the fact that the Church realised she had not merely to do spiritual work—which she had to do primarily—but she had also social obligations. Such a place as that showed that th 3 Church was alive and he hoped that in days to come she would be more alive t 3 her responsibilities. A Church House offered opportunities of intercourse between the clergy and laity. Me felt that in days gone by the clergy had stood off from the laity ; they had not mixed np with the laity, but if they did they would understand more of the layman's mind and the layman would see more of the clergy's mind. By the rubbing of shoulders together and by comparing notes many differences of opinion would be minimised end they would gain immensely in their spiritual and social life. (Applause.) Everything that made for the good of man's physical and intellectual being ultimately made for the good of his spiritual being and, after all, the reason they were promoting social sod educational life was beceuse they were aiming at something higher —the bringing of people into the Kingdom of Christ. (Applause). In proposing a vote of thanks to the Archdeacon for his address Mr. Herbert Woods said they wanted the Church House to be the borne of all Church work and life In Warrington and neighbourhood.

HARVEST FESTIVALS.

WARRINGTON PARISH CHURCH.

This church presented a beautiful appearance on Thursday evening, when the harvest thanksgiving services had commenced. An excellent supply of flowers, fruit, corn and vegetables was forthcoming and the ladies of the congregation had arranged them with great artistic taste. The altar, font, pulpit and clergy stalls, received special attention. There was a large congregation at Evensong, when the preacher was the Rev. Albert Baines, vicar of St. Helena. The other clergy taking part in the service was the Rector (Bev. Canon Willis) and the Rev. J. M. Buckmaster. The music was rendered in that reverent and expressive manner always associated with the Parish Church cLoir, the anthem being " Fear net, 0 land" (Goss). Mr. .1 Poole sang the nib very effectively. Mr F AI Darby, Mao. Rae., presided at the organ. The preacher took his text from I Samuol vi. 13," And they of Bethshemesh were reap- ing their wheat harvest in the valley ; and they lifted up their eyes and saw the Ark and rejoiced to see it." He said the valley of Bethahemesh woo broad and fruit- ful and its slopes were decked with vines and corn- fields. It must have been upon such a scene that the Psalmist's thoughts were centred when he wrote "The valleys stand so thick with corn that they laugh and sing." Bethshemesh was a valley the name of which denoted a valley of sun- shine. It must have presented a beautiful sight as the men, women and children, were all gathered together reaping the harvest. There was everything to make real happiness and yet the people were not happy. what was the reason ? It was because the Ark of the Lord, which in those days signified the presence of God him- self, had been carried away from them and had been taken to the land of the Philistines. They believed that access to the I rescues of God was limited to the presence of the Ark and that the taking away of the Ark meant also the departure of God from them. That valley of sunshine had therefore bean turned into a valley of shadow. The incident went ts show that materialistic ideas in life could never be really satis- factory in themselves while everything in life was dependent upon something else, so in turn we were all dependent upon God, and it was for us, who had this year experienced a harvest several degrees in advance of previous years, to seek out the Kingdom of God. When the people of the valley lifted up their eyes and beheld the Ark once more restored to them, they were moved to rejoicing. So it should be with us, we should lift up our eyes to seek the Lord. The collections were in aid ot the Assistant Clergy Fund, for which £5O is required. The at nrices will be continued to-morrow (Sunday.) The preacher in the morning will be the Rev. Canon Stevenson, and in the evening the Rev. Canon Wainwright (St. John's, Altrincham.) ST. PETER'S CHURCH.

The harvest festival was concluded on Sunday when I large congregations assembled. At the morning service the sermon was preached by tbe Rev. T. T. Lee-Joaea. The choir sang the anthem " Blessed be the name of the Lord" (Maunder), the solo being creditably sung by Mr. Reuben Bennett. In the afternoon, the Vicar (the Rev. B. Percival) conducted a children's service and gave an address. The Ray. Horace Gray occupied the pulpit at the evening service, and preached to crowded congregation. The choir sang "Praise the Lord, 0 Jerusalem " in a pleasing manner, and Master E. Walker" was heard to advantage in the solo. Mr E A Pedmore presided at the organ. ST. " At each service special hymns and anthems were rendered by the choir, Miss Standish presiding at the organ. WORKINGMEN'S MISSION, BANK QUAY.

Good congregations attended the harvest thanksgiving services held at Bold-street Wesleyan Church last Sunday. The church was tastefully decorated, there being an abundant supply of fruits, corn, flowers and vegetables. fhe Rev. E. Morgan preached in the morning and said that the harvest was a perpetual reminder of God's goodness to us. The Rev. H. Middlewood conducted the service in the evening and spoke on the constancy of the love of God. Suns love that Jesus Christ had for us ought to be emulated in our love for Him and our fellowmen. The choir gave a splendid interpretation of the anthem " Awake thou that sleeps." BEWSEY-ROAD WESLEYAN CHURCH.

The harvest thanksgiving services were held at the Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church on Sunday.ln addition to the usual services the cantata "A song of thanksgiving" was rendered by the choir in the afternoon. The Rev. NV. A. Beckett preached in the morning from the text "It came to pass that on the Sabbath Day Ile was going through the corn-fields and His disciples were accompanying Him," Ile said that there was no incon- sistency between a Sabbath walk and Sabbath worship. We could conceive that a walk in the presence of Jesus was conducive to Christian worship ant wonderfully assisted on in maintaining the sprit of the Caristian Sabbath. In passing let hiru say that when so many were making the Sabbath a holiday instead of a holy-day it behoved us to be watchful of the follies of such licence. It would be to our irreparable less as a nation and country if we were to exchange our Christian Sabbath for that of tha Coa- -1 tioent. Continuing, he said there was nothing incon- ' gruons about a devout lover of God being. devout over of nature. A sincere religious belief did not incapacitate us for sincere study of nature's fields. It was the privilege of the Father's child to understand , the wonderful things there %ere to be discovered and that might be underatood as they were revealed in the natural world. What a lover of nature Jesus Christ , was ! How keen His vision I His pure and undimmed insight enabled Him to penetrate into the secrets of the field through which He walked. , Mach depended upon the power of vision. One went about in the world and saw very little. Another went over the same ground and saw more than be could describe. A Wordsworth, or one with great insight, saw in nature things that lay too deep for te ITS while the other, lacking sympathetic insight, saw little beyond no meta marketable value. We saw in the corn- fields God's provision for man's needs. God had furnished grain for the sustenance of the human race and where there was a scarcity in one part of the world there was more than enough in another part to balance things. God always provided for His great family and the voice that came to us at every passing harvest time was, " God cares." Each harvest time was a proof of God's faithfulness, and of the fact that He had not forgotten us. The cornfield bore witness to the permanent and inseparable connection of human industry and Divine blessing. "It is Gad that giveth the increaae," but always in conjunction with man's labour. The harvest was also a standing witness against thriftlessness, bad farming and laziness. Con- tinuing, Mr. Beckett said that God was interested in all that cAnaerned us. His attention was not confined to the House of God and ecclesiastical matters, and let us once and for all banish that atheism which practically excluded God from the concerns of everyday life. During the service the trio "While the earth remaineth " was creditably sung by Misses Hilda Hipkiss, Edwardes, and Wardle. Mr. JAMES POVEY presided is the afternoon, when the cantata" A song of Vianksgiving " (Asunder) was rendered in the presence of a fair congregation. The choir gave a delightful interpretation, the strength and sweetness of all th 3 parte being adequately brought out. Mr. Hipkiss, the able ocnductor, was happy in his choice of soloists—Misses Hipkiss, Hilda Hipkiss, lit Jones, Edwardes, Wardle, Winstanley, Messrs Hannan, Tynan, A Pearce. Mr NV Pearce presided at the organ in his usual efficient manner.

The Rev E MonoAN in the course of his sermon in the availing slid that one of the most beautiful things about the life of Jesus Christ was his intense admira- tion of the beauties of nature. He was not like a certain great saint who, when he went out for a walk abut his eyes so that the beauty of material things should not divert his thoughts from spiritual matters. To Jesus the fields were His study where He thought and meditated, His oratory where lie prayed His sanctuary where He worshipped God. The anthem " For ever 0 Lord" was sung, Mr. A. Pearce being the soloist.

MESSRS. PEARSON AND KNOWLES.

BUSINESS AND POLITICS AT THE ANNUAL MEETING.

Handsome Profits Despite the Chancellor.

The 38th annual meeting of the shareholders cf the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co., Ltd., was held at the company's offices, Bewsey-road, Warrington, on Tuesday at 12 noon. Mr. J. S. Harrnood Banner, 11. P. (chairman of the Board of Directors) presided, and the other directors present were Messrs. William H. Blackly (deputy-chairman) John J. Sleekly, Richard Broacher W. Peter Rylands and Herbert S. Blackly. The report, which has already appeared in our columns, and the balance sheet, were taken as read.

===== 0002601_19110930_art0061.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. lIARMOOD BANNER'S REVIEW.

The CRAIRMAN,iII moving the adoption of the report and statement of accounts, said ; The directors are glad to meet you to-day with a statement which, under all the circumstances of the year, we think may be con- sidered satisfactory. (Hear, hear.) We shoe a better profit than last year, and vre are able to pay yon a slightly better dividend, besides putting away consider- able balance to reserve and paying the increased amount which is required for the dividend on the new prefer- ence shares issued during the year, and which amonnts to about .£2,000. This year has not been without its adverse conditions, though, of course, nothing in com- parisonwith the ditficultie3 since 30th June—in Morocco troubles and railway strikes. The Miners Eight Hours Bill has reduced the wages earned by the men and adversely affected both the production and cost of coal. It has also in the Lanc shire district given advantages to which the facilities of the railway companies have, in my opinion, unfairly added in the importation of coal from the Yorkshire district both for home and export purposes. Lancashire is an old coalfield, and has its own peculiarities, both of wages, customs and qualities of fuel, and the Miners Eight Hours Bill and the Coal Mines Bill now before Parliament affect Lancashire production and costs more than the more modern collieries and qualities of coal in Yorkshire. In fact I have heard an M.P. speaking of the new Coal Mines Bill state that the Yorkshire collieries would shut up Lancashire collieries by the new regulations. Well, we have a stubborn way with us in Lancashire, and I think we will hold our own. In the iron and wire trades we have been fairly busy and our profits are larger than last year. The only new feature in the balance sheet is the item of the new six per cent capital, of which £105,768 was paid on the 90th June. She whole sum of £140,000 is now paid up. The amount is represented on the other side of the account by an increase of the sum in cash and bills in hand and at bankers from £81,574 last year to £150,281 this year, and by an addition in the amount of our investments of .£31,002, the shares we have so far taken up in the Partington Steel and Iron Co., Ltd. Now as regards our profits, you will observe that we are writing off all our additions, a sum of £19,500, which makes up with our usual provisions, a very handsome sum of depreciation. We are increasing the reserve fund by £15,000 out of profits, though per contra we are taking out of reserve the cost of the new issue which we might rightly have spread over a perlod,and the balance carried forward is £13,359. I think the shareholders have every reason to consider the balance sheet most satisfactory.

A HINT TO COAL-USERS.

I think I may say, notwithstanding our anxieties about labour, that we are starting the year well. The demand for coal is active, and I should recommend every consumer, trader as well as householder, to till up his stock or c-llars to their fullest capacity, so as not to starve in shard and cold winter, if,as is undoubtedly possible, a universal strike of coal miners should take place, especially as with the new systsm of sympathetic strikes difficulties in transport are sure to arise and make stocks at collieries useless. I may say that with the spirit of conciliation, which, and for so long, has actuated miners and mine owners I ant hopeful differences may not reach the extremities which' think most unwisely are so often discussed and suggested in the Press. I should like miners particu- larly to remember that it is their own mining legisla- tion which is reducing wages, not owners' legislation, and not put the saddle on the wrong horse. One point especially es regards the field rates of coal where excessive rates are asked to make up the reductions in wages caused by the Miners Eight Hours Bill, and we ourselves have four sesms idle wanting labour in con- sequenc4. The Moss Hall Colliery C mosey is fast being reinstated, though I regret to say the expense has 6(.30 very heavy. We have also finished the equip- ment of the Maypole plt with new screening and electrical plant, and should this winter be reaping a return. The iron trade also in our works is keeping us busy. We have, like almost everyone else, had our differences with labour, but I think I may say they have been satisfactorily adjusted, and, in all our branches of work—engineering and manufacturing—we have reason to be satisfied with the amount of work on hand.

THE NEW STEIL .WORE,

. . Now, with regard to the project of manufacturing steel. to which I referred at our last annual meeting. The Partington Steel and Iron Co. has, as you know, lee u formed. The Board has secured the services, as

managing director of the. new work'', of .Mr Artlor.H

Cooper, who bee had intimate experience in the management and development ot the iron and steel industries in the Cleveland district extending over —. • . . .

msny years Slag, his appointment, Mr, ,oper, in company with other of the directors, has visited many of the most important steel works, both in this country and abroad, and long and careful thought has been devoted to the design of the new works on lines follow- ing the latest and best practice which has stood the test of experience, both in this and other countries. As one illustration, I may point out that the whole of the power required to drive the works, ssy upwards of 20,000 horse power, wi I be developed entirely from the sva.st.e gases frer.o the coke ovens and the blast furnaces.

-4-o-riW;;;

primarily intended f.r the production of the steel required by the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Company and Rylands Brothers. it in the intention of the Board to provide also for the production of speclal qualities ol steel to meet the requirements of the Sheffield market and other consumers of high-class steels.

EMPLOYER AND EMPLOYED.-THE DISTRIBUTION OF

===== 0002601_19110930_art0062.txt | included=true | included =====
PROFITS.

Now I have finished the story of the year, but there are one or two remarks I should like t 3 make before sitting down, and I will do it as abortly as possible, on the relations, between employer and employed and cn the Insurance Bill now before Parliament. Now, first I will give you a few fignres to coasider,and which I hope our workmen will digest too. We employ in all our departments 9,417 men, an i we pay annually a total of wages .6631,140. The number of shareholders in the company is 1,161. The dividend distribuied this year both on the preference and ordinary shares is .£39,086. Tact dividend to the ordinary shareholder is 44,400. All other profi s are rr quired for the mein- tenance of the c3mpany's property and the necesiary extensions to keep it th a high style of efithiency. Now, on the labour question, the first point to consider is, which are the greatest philanthropiats to the labour classes? The politicians. amongst whom I must classify all of his Majesty's Ministers, late or present, Liberal or Conservative, most of whom have never done a day's manufacturing or trading in their lives, and are merely

===== 0002601_19110930_art0063.txt | included=true | included =====
-PROFESSORS OF THFORINS.

or amateurs—(bear, bear)—are ready to give panaceas and remedies for every complaint ; principally, vote- catching doles, costing themselves nothing. (Hear, hear.) Experienoe, the true economic teat, is nothing to them. Are they the true philanthropists? Is the benevolent man who says, " Poor Fellows," when a strike is on, and contributes a donation to a soup kitchen of £5O or £100? Or are the investors i 3 a company like this, trusting their money to labour, pro- viding the facilities for labour to make itself effective, and finding maintenance and livelihood for 9,117 men and their families ? (Hear, hear and applause.) It is absolutely necessary for workmen to take these facts to their inmost consciences and test them. It was stated in the Royal Commission that wages most come before dividends. How about £631,180 wages and £21,400 dividend ? This is our own ii.starme as you know, and we represent many like companies. (Herr, hear). There is one other point before leaving this question to proceed to the discussion of the Insurance Bill. We pay a total of rates and toxes to Government, Imperial £10,812 and Municipal £12,611, together 03,651. Are we entitled to the protection of the State, to be free, ourselves and our men, to carry on business without let or hindrance, without injury to person or property, or have we forfeited protection and the rights to our liberty ? At present no relianoe con be placed on the duty of the Government to help us if difficulties arise, notwithstanding the vast sums we pay and the burdens of taxation we endure. It would almost seem

and an employer merely a slave to be tortured to dis- close and surrender his savings like any barbarous atate in Central Africa.

..... The Insurance Bill now before Parliament calls for considerable comment, on which I should like to any first that I have no objection to the principle, but there is no reason why employers should be subject to such special and exceptional treatment as they are in this Bill, besides paying their fall quota of rates and taxes. NJW there are two phases by which it affec's this company. In the first place we have always kept clear of a debenture debt, or fixed debt. They had all noticed with considerable regret and sickening of heart the continual unrest in the world, and chiefly in the ranks of labour. The workers of many countries were to some extent being misled with the idea th,.t a general strike would result in soma form of social revolution and in a permanent improvement of their condition. Strikes and the widespread labour unrest were signs and symptoms that there must be

SAETEIING" RADICALLY WRONG IN ALL THE CIVILIZED COUNTRIES,. . . .

and it behoved all parties to approach the problem in no spirit of partizenship, but to realize that it aas really a national question. Unless the foundations of a building were sound and true, no matter h3w beautiful the architecture might be, the entire ed,fize would crumble into dust. Unless the general body of the people bad a reasonable opportunity to lice a humane, happy and prosperous life, no nation could call itself really happy while such sores ani diseases were eating at its roots. A general strike meant that workers in trades which were the foundation of all civilized communities—the miners, railway men, iron workers, and builders in large cities—should cease work simultaneously for the purpose of stopping industry and crippling and paralysing Uncle. Some of the closest observers of labour disturbances, especially on the Continent, were issuing a word of warning, because they did not want the workers of any country to be misled into thinking that the adoption of such methods ' would establish a system of permanent jostle?. Any movement to be of value, most be based on the reasoned conviction of large masses of people, and it seemed to him a grossly mistaken idea to imagine that a general strike would be the solutio3 of all their troubles. The very first people to feel the stress and strain of • stoppage of transport and production would be the workers themselves. A navel Harvest Festival was held on Sunday last at the Church of St. Magnus the Martyr, Billingsgate. In addition to the usual corn and wheat and other produce of the land there was the harvest of the sea. D3W3 the centre aisle and araund the font were arranged boxes containing almost every fish known to the housewife, including Saimaa, haddock, hake, halibut, plaice, turbot, herrings, sprats, whiting, soles crabs, and lobsters. Ms fish had been presented by dealers in Billingsgate Market. and after the tlervice was distributed to the local hospitals.
