===== 0002601_19110930_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
FROM ' LABOUR'S STANDPOINT.

By Philip Snowden, M P.

ARE PICKETING AND STRIKES TO BE MADE ILLEGAL? There is no reason to believe that the Government intend to do something in regard to the proposals which have been put

f ;ward by Si; Ctlarles Macara, for Concilia- tion in lAbour disputes. The announcement that it was intended to call a Conference immediately at the Board of Trade, of repre-

sentatives of employers and workmen to con- skier the matter has been officially described as" premature," but that may be taken to

indicate that the announcement has some foundation. It is hinted in some quarters that there may be legislation during the autumn session for the establishment of some system of compulsory conciliation, and if any- thing of Pm sort is pro osed it is not improbable thst it will be passed in view of the present panic-stricken state of public opinion.

AN ATTACK ON PICKETING. There is a tremendous movement going on in very influential quarters for the repeal of the Trades Disputes Act of 1906. It is almost impossible to conceive of the Govern- ment attempting to do any such thing, but if they were so i 1-advised as to make the attempt it is not at all improbable that it inight succeed, for recent labour troubles have terrorised the employing class, and political differences are forgotten in their common anxiety to protect themselves against this menace which has appeared. If the Govern- ment were to endeavour to take away or to limit the right of peaceful picketing it is inconceivable to think what would happen in the present restless state of the working class.

I do not for a moment think that the Government will listen to the terror- stricken shrieks of those who are demand-

ing the repeal of the law of peaceful picket- ing, but I do think it is very likely that we may have legislation rushed through in the autumn giving the Government more power to intervene in labour disputes ; and it may be that a suggestion which is being made will be adopted, namely, that workmen who will not in the first place submit their grievance to a Conciliation Board shall be deprived of the legal right to picket. To do that would be a blunder as fatal almost as to repeal the Trades Disputes Act. The Trade Unionists may be wiling to accept some scheme of compulsory Conciliation as a first step, but it is perfectly certain that they are not willing to have the right to strike taken away from them, if the decision of a Con- ciliation Board is unsatisfactory. A MODERATE BEGINNING.

As I have often pointed out any scheme of legal conciliation for this country will have to be of a very moderate character at first. The Trades Union Congress resolution upon Mr. Will Crooks' Bill must not be taken too seriously. Not one delegate in twenty who condemned the Bill, had ever seen the outside of it. The Manifesto of the General Federation of Trades Unions on this Bill is one which can carry no conviction, because of its wholesale condemnation. It describes the Bill in effect as bad in every word and clause, and as being one which may have come from an Employers' Federation. This is grotesque misrepresentation of the Bill. It is a Bill which has many good points; and what is more it is a Bill very much on the lines of legislation the trade unions will have to take before long; because there is no doubt about it that the country will

demand legislation which will compel both parties to a dispute to try to come to a settle- ment by negotiations before resorting to a strike or lock-out.

TWO SERIOUS QUESTIONS.

There are two matters on which the trade unions have got to make up their minds, and such extraordinary incidents as the Irish railway strike must force some decision on these two points. They are these : First, do trade uoionists while demanding recogni- tion reserve the right to "down tools" at a moment's notice; and, second, do they accept the new idea of the sympathetic strike carried to the extent of outside trades ceasing work, though they have no grievance themselves, and are working under an agreement? If the trade unionists give an affirmative answer to these questions, then, [ say to them frankly and fearlessly, that they are taking up a position, and demanding a liberty which will neither be granted nor tolerated in any country where there is a system of government and law. No individual workman or body of workmen ought to be compelled to work under conditions which are not acceptable, but society will fall to pieces, and a state of anarchy will exist if there is to be no such thing as legal contracts and social obligations. The workman is not willing to give up the . .

right to strike. He cannot agree to any restriction of the right to peaceful picketing. But as the workman is not the only person affected by the strike, he will have to give some consideration to the other interested parties, and the least they are entitled to demand is that before either the employers or the workmen bring a trade to a standstill they shall meet together and try to settle the dispute. THE MANCHESTER SCHEME.

It may be that I do not understand the scheme put forward by Sir Charles Macara` but so far as I do it seems to me hardly strong enough to meet the need. His pro- posal is that there shall be an Industrial Tribunal set up which shall be composed of the most experienced men connected with the great industries, both employers' and work- men's representatives. It is intended that this Industrial Tribunal shall be confined to some half-dozen of the great trades. This scheme assumes that there must be strong organisations on each side, organisations strong enough to enforce obedience to any agreement which may be entered into by the representatives of the trades concerned. Sir Charles Macara assumes rather too readily that there is little fear of agreements being repudiated by either party if each has a strong organisation. There have been many instances within the last two years which do not support that assumption. POPULAR INFLUENCE.

There is no compulsion in Sir Charles Macara's proposal to refer an impending dis- pute to the Industrial Tribunal. He seems to think that the pressure of public opinion would be sufficient to induce both workmen and employers to seek the assistance of this Tribunal before resorting to a stoppage of work. Again, recent experience does not support that assumption. But it may be if the Industrial Tribunal were in existence there would be a restraining influence against precipitate stoppages which is not felt at present. One thing iscertain,namely, that if the parties could be got to submit their dispute to this Industrial Tribunal, and if this Tribunal was so constituted as to command public confidence, its decision would

carry great weight, and which ever party refused to accept it would have public sympathy against it if it resorted to a stoppage of work. WHAT TO AVOID.

Whatever may be done must not be too drastic. L must not carry the appearance of being a limitation of the freedom and right of labour to fight for better conditions. The power of labour to enforce concessions by industrial action is at the best weak com- pared with the power of organised capital. 13ut legislation which provided the means for the reference of disputes first of all to a fair Industrial Tribunal would strengthen and not weaken the power of labour. The greatest asset which the workers can have is the sympathy of the public. When strikes take place now without any previous attempt at Concifiation, the public are in the dark as to the merits of the disputes. There are all sorts of conflicting statements, but all of them prejudiced or biassed. If an impartial enquiry could first be instituted, and if the report of that enquiry were made public, it would be a justification for a strike if the employers refused conces- sions to the workmen which the Tribunal had decided were just and reasonable. We are in very troublesome times. Cool heads are needed, but the difficulty is that they are hard to find when the blood is up. Certain recent happenings have made strikes popular in certain quarters. On the other hand these successes have aroused a desire to restrict the legal powers of the workers. The problem to be solved is to devise means by which the just advantages which can now only be won by the brutal method of the strike may be secured as concessions to the appeal of reason and justice.

CHILDREN'S CORNER, Conducted by UNCLE CALEB.

HOW CATS FALL.

A scientist has constructed an ingenious model to show why a cat in falling always alights on its feet. This model, roughly speaking, consists of a cardboard cylinder wherein are stuck four rods to serve for legs, together with a tail devised on similar principles. The object of the experiment is to show that a cat's peculiar faculty depends on the rotation of its tail with sufficient vigor. This faculty is one especially developed by climbing and leaping animals, such as members of the cat tribe, monkeys, squirrels, rats, and most lemurs. As mentioned, the tails plays an important part in the turning process. According to the investigator, all tree-inhabiting monkeys have long tails, and there is not the slightest doubt that these tails are of great aid to all climbers in enabling them to turn in the air. The tail also serves as a balancer, as evidenced in the case of a squirrel, which may be seen walking along a tightly stretched wire or string, swinging its tail from side to side, much after the manner of a tight-rope walker balancing himself with a pole. Pussy manages to turn in the air by giving her tail a vigorous twist and so bringing her body into the right position.

Yes, you may experiment with pussy; but remember that she has nerves and feeling.

PILES CAUSED HIM 12 YEARS' MISERY.

Manchester Sufferer Permanently Cured by Zam-Buk.

Mr. William White is a happier man to-day than he has been for twelve long years, for Zam-Buk has com- pletely lid him of itching piles that made his life miserable. Mr. White, who is a gas-meter tester, lives at 20, Derby-street, Lower Broughton,Mancbeeter, where he told his remarkable experiences to a reporter. " It most be twelve years since the piles began to trouble me," said Mr. White. "At first I didn't know what was wrong, but I was CO plagued with the itch- ing that I quickly had medical advice. The doctor's ointments, however, had no good effect at all ; instead, I found the piles weedily getting bigger and more painful. When I went to bed or got extra warm the piles itched worse than ever, and I made mrelf quite sore through rubbing and scratching them. At times was so bad that I couldn't go to my work. . - .

The doctor then told me that nothing could cure me. But I was in ruch torture that I felt I most try something. So, after rrading how Zam-Buk bad cured another workman who had suffered for many a weary year, I sect for a box of the balm.

===== 0002601_19110930_art0080.txt | included=true | included =====
PILGRIMAGES

FAR AFIELD.

Luther and the Wartburg.

If there were but one place in Germany that the good Protestant were permitted to see, there is little doubt that his choice would fall upon the castle of Wartburg. In a striking and peculiar way the fine old fortress is associated with the Reformation struggle, and because of this will be famous as long as it stands. _ _

Perched high above the city of Eisenbach its two square towers have stood like sentinels for eight and a half centuries and have looked down upon scenes strangely diverse and wonderful.

Its origin is interesting. At the time— perhaps the very year—when the Conqueror was beginning his attack upon England, a hunter was pursuing the stag in the Thuringian forest. The hunt carried him to the top of the hill, and as he emerged from the trees at the summit the beauty of the scene laid hold on him. Instantly there rose to his lips a resolve, "Wart' Berg, du sollst Or eine Burg werden." ("Guardian of the Hill, thou shalt surely become a stronghold for me.") Four years later, in 1070, his wish was fulfilled, and his castle took its name from his first words " Wart Berg."

===== 0002601_19110930_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
WARTBERG MEMORIES.

Quite apart from Martin Luther the castle would be famous. Its large-hearted builder was a friend of poetry and letters, and gathered here the most notable bards of I the Middle Ages. For years on end he sheltered and encouraged the singers of Europe at a time when few could see the value of their work. And they, in turn,

LUTHER'S ROOM IN WARTBURG.

The chamber is situated in the portion of the castle on the extreme left of the accompanying illustration, distinguished by its two chimneys. The wall, hacked by relic-hunters, is discernable in the right hand corner.

immortalised him and his glorious home, and made them famous by their songs. It was at Wartburg that the Battle of the Bards" was said to have taken place. and though the story is pure legend, it is valuable as showing how largely the castle was the home of the scholars of medieval days.

In one corner of the room the wall looks as though it had been attacked with a pickaxe. Formerly there was a black stain on the wall said to be the veritable ink stain, but the relic-hunter chipped it bare. Most obligingly the wardens inked the wall further on to keep up the interest, but it soon became evident that the entire chamber would have to be sacrified. Shorn of thirty square feet of plaster, and the rough stones showing, this corner of the room is far from inviting. Notwithstanding the interesting relics, we imagine that the most reverent visitors will find the view from the window more full of suggestion and inspiration. The glorious landscape spreads scarcely changed since Luther looked on it. Under this window he sat at his oaken table and translated the Bible. He would raise his eyes to look out in fragrant summer and through the snowy winter to find inspiration in the deep valleys and hills spread out before him. By the help of the picture we can imagine we see the resolute eyes looking upon the face of nature; the strong brain behind them busy the meanwhile thinking great thoughts and planning the great battle which meant so much to Europe and the world. SAINT ELIZABETH.

_ The story of" Saint Elizabeth" is also interwoven with that of the Wartburg. Having lost her husband during the Crusades she was turned out of the castle by her cruel brother-in-law, and walked homeless until taken in by her uncle. The old castle is full of memories of" Holy Elizabeth," and because of this is very dear to the German heart.

Yet intcresting as these things are, they certainly would not attract to the Wartburg the thousands who annually pass through its gates. The supreme memory of the grey castle is not in medieval poetry nor in the sainthood of Elizaneth. The great interest centres in the fact that for one brief year it ' sheltered Martin Luther. LUTHER'S IMPRISONMENT.

Most of us know the story. Luther had hurled defiance at the Diet of Worms. Twenty-one days had been given to him in which to leave the country. It was the most critical time in the struggle. Luther was not the man to give way, nor were his enemies likely to relent. The days of grace were slipping away,when one evening a party of horsemen seized him and carried him under cover of darkness to the castle of Wartburg. From friend and foe alike Luther had disappeared. Since the attack was a forcible one it had the appearance of being the act of his enemies, but the plot was not a plot upon his life. It was the attack of a friend. Frederick the Wise had carried him to the lonely castle to save him from the hands of his foes. We can imagine how Luther's active spirit would chafe under the enforced confinement. Strangely, and in a way which no one could have foreseen, that quiet year at Wartburg changed history. A great project seized him. He would translate the Bible into the language of the common people. What Wycliffe and Tyndale did for England, Luther did for Germany. The people then spoke varying dialects. Luther gave them a Bible in rich beautiful language and the language of this book eventually became the common speech of the German people. Luther's translation on this account is by far the most famous book in German history. Yet its spiritual significance was greater than its literary. With the Bible freely circulated the battle of the reformation began to turn in favour of the Reformers. Luther's forced exile was the great providence of his life.

But we are not writing history; we have come to look with keenly interested eyes upon the castle chamber in which he lived. It is only a small room at the south-west corner, and our dim photograph will give the reader an idea of its appearance.

We are shown his bed, the side-posts of which have been cut to pieces by relic hunters. His table, the edges protected from the same tribe by pieces of tin, also remains, and we are shown his wife's wedding ring and other relics.

KING. STREET, MANCHESTER.

There is a fine painting of Luther hanging in the room, and this helps us to conjure up the scene. We can picture him as he lives in this little room. He rises early and pours out his soul in prayer. No man has greater anxieties, and they would crush an ordinary soul, but his faith is greater than his fears. The winter storms burst around him, but he is within strong walls and the thought of his safety inspires him. It takes shape in the form of a great hymn of faith, the hymn which became the battle song of the Reformation— A mighty fortress is our God, A bulwark never failing _-• . .

Luther was iiot free from-spiritual wrest- lings during his confinement, and one familiar story may be quoted in illustration. It was in this room that Luther is said to have thrown his ink-pot at the devil. So real had the evil presence become that he felt him to be there in visible form, and took the ink-pot as a weapon.

There was a lift that worked spasmodically, but on this occasion they ascended by the broad low staircase. It was of stone, very dirty, and destitute of car- pets. On reaching the first floor, they turned down a wide corridor, and Madame Gautier, with an sir of intense pride, flung open the door of a lof i buy sitting-room, stood n the en- trance, raised her hands with • gesture of eestaey, and said: "Behold for yourselves, Madame and Mon• slew'!" The room was spacious, certainly, and the furniture fairly good of its kind. But it had long past its meridian of excellence. The pattern of the carpet was no longer distin- guishable; the windows were as smoky and dirty as the window of Mary's small sitting- room. There was a good deal of furniture of the heavy description. A bugh table, covered with a dirty plush cloth, stood in the centre of the apartment. Over the mantelpiece was the invariable mirror, much tarnished with age and use. A couple of sofas, with broken springs, stood uninvitingly one at each side a tb• fire,?--s;

Mary ordered a fire to be lit; and the room to be cleaned, and then proceeded to inspect the bedroom beyond. It was dark and dis- mal, but M. !taco, in his most courteous French. said that he would venture to secure Ibex apartments for one night in the hope that his friend, Nadir Julaver, a very great gentleman from Teheran, would occupy tlitn. He further desired that the omnibus of the hotel should wait at the quay for the arrival of the boat. Then, having requested Mary to see that the rooms were really at- tended to, and having further desired Madame Gautier to have plenty of food in the house and the best coffee ready for Jula- ver, he went away. Before he went he had a word with Mary.

"I consider the thing accomplished," 'he said. " Nadir Julaver will come here. You will have made his acquaintance in advance. lie will be more than content with his ac- commodation and, if you are a clever woman, you will win your way. But—may I recall to your mind once more, that failure means"— be dropped his voice—"denth !"

The Ring's First Statne.—The first statue af King George V , standing altogether some 30 fret high, was unveiled in the grounds of the Crystal Palace last week, by the Lord Mayor of London. It is the work of Mr. Arthur Rogers, sculptor, and represents his Majesty is his Coronation robes. The figure, 16 feet high, has teen designed, and made in bronzed plaster, during the past three montts, at the Festival of Empire, at the Palace, which is now over. Vhat will become of the statue ore hardly knows, seeing that the Crystal Palace has been catalogued for sole by anction in a few week's time, There has been a good deal of talk of acquiring the Palace, and its beauti- ful grounds as a national memorial to King Edward, end the Lord Mayor took the opportunity afforded by the unveiling ceremony, to trace the inti- mate connection of the Roy ti Family with the Crystal Palace, since it was first erected in Hyde Park for the Exhibition of 1851. It was afterwards opened at Sydenham Hill by Queen Victoria and the Prince Con- sort in 1851. The great misfortune was, that the spot chosen for its re-erection, was not served by a direct main line railway. Although only some 60, 7 miles from the heart of London, it is, in the ordinary way, the beat part of an hour's journey to get to the Palace, and it la the lack of facilities for easily getting there, facilities which in many respects as regards the railway Cos., are little better thsn they were half a century ago, that has brought the Palace to its present financial strs its. •" •

Our Historic Historic Buildings—The Americans, unlike our- selves, and particnlarly Scotsmen, live in a country that has no castles, and that is perhaps one reason why we so often hear that their millionaires contemplate buying tip this or that historic building, in which we ought to take a national pride. Hut it is much easier to tslk of buying up Windsor Castle, or Glastonbury Abbey, and re-erecting it in the States, -stone for stone, and brick for brick, than it ie to do it, and itis too absurd to suggest that the ascient tower of Tattershall Castle, w !lido is 900 feet high, could be removed to the States in this wanner. It hen been announced that an American millionaire has bought it for remove' and re-erection in the States, but the titter, all that remains of the original building, is of brick, huilt in the fifteenth cen- tury, and its walls are in pl10(s fifteen feet thick. No doubt this meagre respsnse was in part due to the scepticism with which the whole affair is regarded, but it affords another evidence of the national indifference that is invariably displasel in the matter nf the preservation of our historic buildings. I Sleep Training.—The fresh air enthusiasts who have been sleeping in the garden during the summer, are now returning to their accustomed quarters in doors. The Bible warns us against the night air, and the bird always sleeps with its head under its wing, but then is no doubt that the doctors are right in saying that we should sleep with open bedroom windows whenever possible. If one's room lancer to a main told noise may make it impos- sible to rest with open windows, and in this age of ever iocrensing motor traffic mothers of young children are advised to train their little ones to sleep through noise and disturbances. The old notion of imposing silence on the household for fear of waking the baby, is, we are told, all wrong. If the child is trained to sleep Welles all kinds of noise, it will he much better able to defy nocturnal distsubances when grown up. At least that is what the sleep specialis's say, although they will find it difficult to get mothers, who as it is, are eccustonied to pass re tless eights, to put this thu ory very thoroughly into practice. Feeding the Itirds.—The experience of the Milhorne farmers who organised a campaign against sparrows and have found a considerable diniiention in the damage to their crops, tends very strnngly to confirm the view that those who say that these birds should be pieced In a category different from that of our beautiful song birds, which cannot be too sedulously protected. The sparrow indeed is very much of a uisance. Among his sine are the facts that he destroys nearly every kind of crops, and not only does little or nothing towards protecting them from insects but drives away swallows and other birds, whose presence would be advantageous to the farmers and those who cultivate gardens. When the weather becomes more severe we shall find kind- hearted people writing to the papers asking housewives to feed the birds, but while the suggestion is honour- able to the writers, at the same time those who have tried, know, that at any rate, in London and the great towns, feeding the birds means only feeding the sparrows, which will not suffer the beautiful birds to COMB near. This FAMO firm originated the harem skirt last spring, and to maintain a rcpulatitn for originality, has this autumn designed a model, which is, to all intents and purpose., a bell- shaped sheath, for as fares the knees it is as tight as the bobble ; below the knees the skirt widens out like a bell, and is distended just above the ankle.s, by means of a wire stiffening. When not carried to the extreme, this skirt is by no means unbecoming, nor is it altogether ungreoefut, and it is undoubtedly capable of modification. An the season advances it will, is all probability, be considerably improved and altered and the result may give us a teeny smart skirt. There is still great diversity of opinion with regard to the width of skirts for the wiser. At present, last spring's styles are worn by smart women, and shown in many of the last houses, and these are very good examples for ordinary folks to follow. The skirt more thin any any other part of womens' attire seems this season to have agitated authorities on dress, and uncertainty and contradiction, characterises statements made by tailors, dressmakers, and fashion papers. All this, however, should prove an advantage for every woman may wens the width that pleases her, and still feel herrielf fashionably clothed. For evening skirts there is a unanimity in style. Except for dancing, a train of sorts is derrigeur. Winter Millinery.—The felt hat of the smooth floe type, is this winter to come into its own again, and, inharmonious as such a mingling of fabrics may sound, it will be trimmed with lace, not only in its natural and string colones, but in every imaginable dyed shade. These latter make very u Elective butterfly bows, one of which frequently constitutes the sole trimming of a fine felt hat. Felt velours, a thick fabric, with a rough eurface, is more suited for seaside weer, the moors and the country. Black velvet ribbon strings are a revival used with every type of hat. Sometimes the ends are tied at the nape of the neck, or one end hangs, instead of in front, over the shoulder. The strings are often threaded through the crown of the hat. Enormone flowers made of velvet and satin, such as gigantic poppies, roses and orchids, with big hLck bows in soft silk, arid of course aigrettes in a variety of shades form the trimmings of autumn and winter hats. Indeed this may be said to be a season of bows, for both on bats and dresses they play an important role. The bows on most of the toques are very big, and look like fly-sway wings. What I read about slippers in last Satur- day's " Women's Clint " brought these to mind. PEDESTRIANISM. I have interviewed more "freak " pedestrians than I can remember by name, though, if called upon, I could reftesh my memory by referense to the inevitable post cards, espies of which were in each case thrust upot me. There win one who trundled a pair of carriage wheels as he went through the country ; and there was a foreigner German, I think —who pushed his wife and child in a kind of bath chair, and told me be had undertaken to thus propel them round the world. Of course, there was a substantial wager involved ; and, equally of course, as already indicated, the family maintryned themselves co route by retailing at a penny each postcards which cost about that per d)zen. Again, owe when I was editing a weekly paper in a Lancashire cotton town, a young fellow who was wo.lling the Union Jack as a vest burst into the place with a story to the like effect, the difference between hie methods and that of the astute continental being that he relied upon newspaper men to supply the wherewithal--a fatal blunder. Then there was that sturdy oil mechanic, Mark All, who, although upwards of NO years of ago, thought nothing of a tour ef the British Isles on foot. Ile would work at his trade on the way, and yet walked 100,006 miles in nine years, passing through five cintinente. INDOMITABLE.

After these came Schilling, win, last week took Warrington in the course of his second walk round the world. When I last encountered him, he stated that he had undertaken to walk 20,000 miles through Great Bi Rain and the Continent during the ensuing three years, this being the :amine et a wager of £5,000. Ile had then walked 2,116 utiles, drawing with his one arm his " airship," weighing ficwts , in ' which he took his meals and slept at nights. His longest walk on one day was 31 miles,die average being 181. His previous walk round the woad was accomplished in ten years. I was informed that the conditions in that instance were that Schilling should start from New York in a paper suit, without a cin in his pocket, and cover the distance in eight years. Unfortunately so many were the difficulties he encountered that he over-ran the time limit, and so lost the wager. All the other conditions however, he faithfully fulfilled. He left New York on August 3rd, 1807, in a suit ingeniously constructed out of newspapers, sad without a cent, in his pockets. lie made money on the road by lecturing and Bering pamphlets and photographs of himself. According to the story he then told, he met with frightful hardships during his ten years' tramp. A thousand miles of the outward journey to Vancouver were made through snow; he nearly died of thirst in South Australia, saving himself by cutting the telegraph wire and so bringing the mounted repair gang to his rescue; he was left for dead by the Boxers during the rising in China; was in prison two- ar.d-a-half months in Turkey; and had many narrow shaves in India. Reminds one of Foster Fraz w's big bicycle run. Other walks Schilling has carried out include Pittsburg to New York, 111 miles, inn nine dun; and Pittsburg to San Fraucbc) and back, 8,900 miles, in nine months and 28 days. Walking enthusiasts hereabouts wee, gready interested it. this confirmed vagrant on the (-mei -12 of his visit to Warrington. These, by-the-bye and in conclusion, take pride in the as ertion, that while every form of exercise has its particular seasons —cricket and tennis and swimming in summer, and football and hockey and gymnastics in winter —there is no close season for their hobby ; there may be better times than others, but all times are available to them. FEEDING OF SCHOOL CHILDREN. That there are aspects of this question that the special pleader reported in your last dtd not feel called upon to mention goes without saying. As I write I have before me a report of the Hoard of Education on the working of the Provision of Meals Act, passed in 1901; This, as readers generally will in all likelihood be aware, gives power to one the rates for the supply of meals, but the limit of rate-aid is the proceeds of a levy of a halfpenny in the A:. As to the recovery of the ciAst of meals it is provided that the authority " shall " charge any parent with the cost of food stupefied "unless they are satisfied that the parent is unable by reason of circumstances other than his own default to pay the amount." There is no specified sum that shall be charged, but whatever the authority fixes the parent must pay subject to the above Con- dition, and the amount may be recovered by legal proceedings from any contumacious person' Omitting all reference to the elaborate statistics which the document contain', I may just state that in a preparatory note to the report Mr. C. A. Selby Big,ge, as principal assistatit secretary of the elementary education branch of the Board's staff, says —"lf children are admitted to meals without , any previous investigation of the pecuniary circum- stances of their parents, then from the economist's i point of view, the failure to recover, or to attempt to recover, any appreciable part of the cost requires justification." fhore are other considerations which I should like to touch upcn. As the report to which , I refer very truly says, a really large proportion of the badly-nourished childrea suffer from unsuitable food rather than iron lack of

means. It is pr ibahly no exaggeration to say that the improvement which could be effected in the phyaique of elementary school children in the poor parts of our large towns, if their parents could be taught or persuaded to spend the same amettot of money as they now spend on their children's food in a more enlightened and sensible manner, is greater than any improvement which could ha effected by feeding them intermittently at the cost of the rates. It may be unppular to write thus; as to that, I do not know —n ogre. After all, though I can tot gt nearly so far as did speakers at the Co-operative 11 .11 meeting last week, I really sympathise with reasonable schemes for the feeding of necessitous children at the pablic expense. The usual quarterly meeting of the Stockton Heath Pariah Council was held on Thursday evening in the I Hail Cafe.—Mr. H. J. Westbrnok presided over a very small attendance, which was no doubt du, to the inclement weather. The business was purely of a routine nature. At the parish meeting there war a fair and representa- tive attendance of ratepayere.—Mr. 11. J. Westbrook, who presided, announced at a meeting of the Runcorn Rural Administrative Sub-Committee, that the request 1 of the Stockton Heath Garden Soc ety had been granted, that there should be horticultural lectures delivered in the parish dueng the winter months. It had also been intimated to him through an influential source that the Mersey Power Company would be disposed to hasten the time of their advent into the parish with electricity supply if some reasonable assurance could he given as to the likely demand. A discussion ensued as to the means of ascertaining the desires of the parish on the matter end it was resolved to ask the Parochial Committee to deal with it by such methods as they deemed fit. Various other parochial matters were discussed including the question of the future maintenance et the trees planted in the streets and roads of the parish. The Chairman pointed out that an effort had been made to spread the cost over the whole rural area but the District Council declined to do that and it was, there- fore, for the parishioners to decide whether they would themselves bear the cost, wnich would have to be raised by a special rate. It was decided to ask the District Council to take over the maintenance of the trees and to charge the pariah w:th the expenditure which would be about £6 or .£7 per annum. The opinion of the meeting was also taken as regards Ike continuance of half yearly pariah meetings. The Chairman mentioned that Stockton Heath was one of the few places where they had half yearly meetings of the parishioners and it had been intimated to him that they should revert back to the annual parish meeting which was customary in similar places. The audience seen.ed to be in favour of having half 'earl, meetings.

Whitley.

CHILD BURNED TO DEATH. NO FIREGUARD. An inquest was held at the Warrington Police Station on Monday by Coroner Brighouse, on the body of Era Themes, the fonr-yaareold daughter of John Thomas, a stockman residing at Whitley lodge, Whitley, who died io the Warrington Infirmary on Friday from the effects of burns. John Thomas said he last sew his daughter alive at eight o'clock on Friday morning the 22nd just, when he left the house to go to work. He left three co Reins in the house, Elsie aged 8 years, Elizabeth aged 6 years and the deceased. His wife bad gone away the previous day to Earleetown. About 10 a.m. on Friday he was working in afield close to the house when his daughter Elsie told him that her sister Eva had been set on fire. He inquired how it had happened and she replied that the deceased was putting some water iato the kettle when her pinafore caught fire. Witne s went in and found the deceased badly burned. She was removed to the Infirmary. In reply to the Coroner witness slid that there was no fireguard. He would have been going back to the house a few minutes after the accident occurred. He earned lie, a week with a csttage free. Coroner : You know that the Act says it lean offence against the law to leave children in a room where there in no protection from the fire. Tabs is the kind of case that the leg;slature intended to meet. Thomas Bell, a sexton. residing at Rose Cottage, Whitley. said that about 10 a.m. on the day in question whilst walking along the road about 100 yards from Whitley Lodge he heard a child scream and then saw decesaed rush out of the house in flames. He imme- diately ran to the child and taking off his court wrapped it round the deceased and rolled her on the grou ad. lie then conveyed her hoe e. Nurse Thompson said that the deceased was admitted to the Infirmary at 1.15 on Friday afternoon and died about 2.45. Ihe child was badly burned all over the body with the exception of the hands sad feet. Toe Coroner : Here is the month of September up to which time we have had fine weather end there has been no need for fires. But as soon as the cold neither comes and the need for a fire is felt it seems to be the beginning of the season fur deaths of this character. He would have to enter that, continued Mr. Brighouse, as the first cass of tha burning season of 1911. It would not have made the alighteat difference in that case if the mother had been at home. The accident might just have happened in the same way, an a mother was compelled to attend to her domestic duties and could not be expected to be always with the children. An Act of Parliament had been passed, and most properly so, compelling the use of fireguards, to prevent those lamentable deaths and the magistrates should not hesitate to make an example, as it was not in his power to do no, ass warning, to elms that the act had to be observed. The Coroner (to the deceasePs father) : Whether you are brought before the magistrates or not you will have to gets fireguard. The jury re.,urned a verdict of' Accidental Death."

CRUELTY TO A HORSE.

WARRINGTON CAB PROPRIETOI RESPONSIBILITY.

At Northwicb, on Tuesday, Amos Chitter, horse- keeper to Mr. Edward Connor, cab proprietor, Warring- ton, was lined .£.5 for allowing a suffering horse to be worked. The evidence showed there was a large raw wound under the saddle, and a partial rupture of the spiral cord. The animal was in a very poor condition, and, in reply to the Bench, who declared that the owner should be reached by some means, Inspector Brain said that Mr. Connor was virtually retired, and guilty know- ledge could not be proved. The Beach still declared that the owner should be saddled with the responsibility, anti hoped he would pay the fine.

TRADE JEALOUSY.

ENDS IN POLICE COURT PROCEEDINOS. An interesting case in which trade jealously was the chief feature was heard at the Borough Court on Thursday before Mr. W. Roberts and other magistrates when two sweet veuclors, Robert Alexander Naylor, Winwich-road end Jane Smart, 32,Queen-street appeared i on a charge of committing a breach of the peace n the market by being of disorderly behaviour, on the 16th inst. Mr. Steel represented Naylor and Mr. Brown appeared for Smart. P.C. Turner said that about 11 p m. on the day in question hesaw the defendants quarrelling lathe market about the price and quality of the sweets they each bad on Bale. There was a very large crowd of people who blocked the passage up. The crowd was also taking their repective sides which resulted in further rows. Inspector Foster said that he was called to the scene, which was one of the worst he had ever witnessed in vie market. Naylor was in such a temper that he thought he would Pave struck the woman, as only a small partition divided the stalls, and the woman Smart was crying. He requested them to be quiet and carry on their business in a proper manner to which Naylor replied that he would carry on his business as be liked as he paid to do to and that it had nothing to do with the police. Mr. Steel : This has all come about through trade jealousy. There bad been a re-arrangement of the market stalls and unfortunately the two defendants who were engaged in the same business were brought together. The defendant Naylor sold his sweets at a lower cost than Smart and in consequence had suffered a considerable amount of abuse from Mrs. Smart. He suggested that the case might be adjourned with a view to the Markets Committee being approached to see if they could be separated, for as long as they were together the business would not be carried on in a satisfactory manner. It was that keen competition which brought them to such a pitch of excitement. If only one of the defendants was bound over the matter would not be nettled and he therefore asked that both might be bound over. Mr. Rymer Young : It is as bad as the Johnson- Wells case. Robert Naylor said that there was not n large crowd. Mr. Brown said that this conduct of the defendant Naylor was only a repetition of other Saturday nights and It had got so intolerable that they intended if it did not cease, to go to court with it. The defendant Naylor used most obscene language to Smart, and his conduct had so upset her on previous occasions that she had been compelled to send for a stimulant. The Chairman said that the Bench were unanimously of opinion that the defendant Naylor should be hound over to keep the peace in the sum of T 5 and he should also have to pay the costs. The cane against Smart would be dismissed. They also thought that the Markets Committee should, at the earliest possible time separate the parties. HUSBAND'S SHOCKING CONDUCT. DRINK THE CAUSE.

A shocking case of a husband's conduct and cruelty when in drink was heard at the Borough Court on Friday before the Mayor and other magistrates, whoa Reginald Albert Gittoes, 22, Alexandra-street, a motor cycle mechanic, was summoned by his wife, Mary Gittoes, a teacher at King-street Roman Catholic School, for being an habitual drunkard, and asking for a separcion order.—Mr. Steel represented the com- plainant, and stated that it was only with great reluct- ance that the case h d been brought before the magis- trates that day. When the Act was put into operation exempting married women from erthool, the complainant on account of her husband's conduct was granted an exception, as it was necessary on account of the defendant's drunken habits for her to obtain is livelihood. The parties were married in January, 1899. In September 1910 the complainant interviewed him (Mr. Steel) who sent a letter to the defendant warning him against proci sidings if he did not mend his ways. He made all kinds of promises and for a time kept to them, but becoming unemployed in Jenuary 1911 he fell away. lie received certain compensation from the firm with wnom he had been employed and he again i commenced business on his own account. From that 'time until the present day he has been practically in ' one continual state of drunkenness. The complainant Iliad had to maintain the defendant, herself and their child. Whilst in drink he used the most frightful language and it was found necessary to send the child to a convent at Bolton but the defendant in one of his drunken fits went to the convent and after creating a scene brought the child away. The complainant's life had been intolerable and in consequence it was impossible for her to follow her duties. On the morning he received the summons he acted like a madman. He threw the summons in the complainant's face and then threw it out of the window while later he nailed it on the outside of the door. On Monday last he arrived home at 11 p.m. and bursting his stay into the complainant's room assaulted her while he also assaulted the little girl who went between them. On one occasion when the child was holding a crucifix the defendant pointing to it said" You don't believe that there ever was such a man as that do you." He had also been to the school where the defendant was employed and upon one occa- sion tha headmistress had to send for is constable to remove him. Frances Corcoran sister of the complainant stated that the complainant had continually to seek refuge in her mothers house to escape from the defendant some- times as late as II o'clock at night. The defendant had also followed the complainant there and created scenes. 'lbs defendant asked that the case might stand adjEurned to enable him to obtain witnesses who were at present out of town. The Caairman . You have treated the summons with contempt and are not worthy of an adjournment. Photo, Tonge MR. J. MANSFIELD COBB (resident engineer), AND MR. ALFRED THORNE (contractor), SUPERINTEND- ING THE PREPARATIONS FOR THE PILE-DRIVING CEREMONY ON THURSDAY NEXT.

In the foreground will be observed one of the concrete piles, which is about 20 feet in length, 14 inches in diameter, and weighs 28 cwt. The old faCioned wooden pile has been superseded by the above type, which is built up of steel bars and spirals reinforced with concrete.

After many disenssiors and wranglings in the 1 Council Chamber, and a memorable fight in the 0 m- I mittee rooms of both Houses of Parliament extending over two Sessions, the much desired reconstruction of Warrington Bridge has now been pm in a pracic .1 form, and the first two piles of the new structure ere to be driven next Thuisday, October stb, by his I Worship the Mayor and by Alderman Smethu.st, the chairman of the Bridges Committee. As the construction of the bridge will be of a novel and interesting character, the following notes may be I interest to our readers; and as a preliminaty statement we may mention that this new bridge will ; be the fourth bridge that has been built across the Mersey at this point. The first bridge was built of stone in 1490 by the first Earl of Derby as a compli- ment to his Royal relative King Henry VII. ; the second bridge was of timber, designed by Mr. Harrison of Chester, and wan erected in 1812; the third bridge was the existing structure cpened in 1837, and christened the Victoria Bridge. The new bridge, as designed by Mr. John J. Webster, M. Inst. C. E. of Westminster, will consist of one arched span cf 134 f t., clear between the abut- manta, and a width between the parapets of 80ft., and made up of a roadway 60ft. wide, and two footpaths loft, wide each. The structure will be constructed entirely of reinforced concrete. The Considerre system of reinforcement has been adopted in the construction of the bridge, consisting of a number of steel soirale encircling the main longitudinal reinforcing steel code; the spirals having the double effect of makieg an efficient bond to the concrete, and considerably increasing the compressive resistance cf the concrete enclosed within the spirals. Eight main ribs will carry the roadway, and two outer ribs the footways, vertical poste on the ribs supporting the main longitudinal and transverse beams of the roadway deck or fleorirg upon which is laid Jarrah wood blocks for the roadway and granolithic slabs for the footways, the kerba of Rawtenstall stone. The ribs are parabolic in form, their under- side at the centre of the span being 17.42 ft. above low-water level, which is Oft. above ordnance datum, while the centre line of the rib at the sprirging is Bit. Gin, above low-water level. The underside of the ribs at the springing is curved eons to make the fsce of the abutment tangential to the curve. The depth of the main ribs normal to the cei tre line at a point Zift. from the abutment is 3rt. the width being Mt. Din, and the section at the centre is 21t. 9in, depth by 3ft. Din, wide, the ribs having cross bracing at i intervals. The font of the ribs s carried through into the abutments oreuing out into well bracketed counterforts which are attar heel to the base plate. The base plate is vertical fnr a depth of sft, at theland side, and at an angle of 30deg to the horizon for a length if 10ft., and is caniid forward to form a horizontal platform 12ft. wide for the dead weight of the earthwork, the face next the river being vertical to form a liter wall 12. ft. high. The whole is monolithic, and reinfo ced through- out with steel rods of varying diameter. The base plate rests upon nine rctagonal reinforced concrete pilee driven normal to tl.e fare of the base plate, the centre of the piles being in line with the resultant of the dead had stres-es ; as the live load is small com- pared with the dead load, the combined resultant will not deviate sericusly from the line of the piles, the horizintal thrust being partly taken by the vertical face of the bedplate. The bedplate is Bin, thick and is well bracketed to the counterfot ta. The concrete piles are of the Considere type, the spiral reinforce- ment protecting the pile to a large extent from the action of the falling monkey when driving,doing away with the necessity of a sawdust pillow to protect the head, and -consequently requiring fewer blows fur driving the pile. The vertical steel reinforcing rods are carried Into the abutment, an that any tendency to slide from the horizontal thrust will be resisted by the sides of the piles aid the earth pressure ; and this, coupled with the horizontal frit lion of the base plate and the resietarce of the vertical face rest the land, makes the stability of the abutments under sty condition of loading ribs lutelpr certain. The top and bottom of rekfmeements of the a amn ribs consists of s'eel bars running longitudinally the whole length of the ribs and cf very in,: diameter and number according to the stresses. Spirals in the top and bottom of the rib en.rircle the longitudinal main reinforcing rodaall being bound together with hatgets and stirrupe. The spirals are 9in, in diameter by 0-16 in. and lin. diameter, pitched 2in. The vertical poets are 15in. by 10in, the reinforcing rods bring bonded into the ribs at the bottom and the longitudinal girders at the top. The longitudinal girders are 14in. by Din, reinforced thriughout. Transverse girders, 10in, by in, suppot t the floor Sr deck, which is CO,in. thick, reinMiced top and bottom to take the stnsses due to any rendition of load, especially for the condition of alternate bays being loaded and thus throwing tensile stress on the t .p of the iLtercenfins bay, a condilion which frequently exists with ordinary wheel traffic, but ehich is very often neglected when designing the floor. The floor under the footpatl s is of special construc- tion, prerared to receive three water pipes lft. Oie, in diameter, three gas pipes lft. 6in, in diameter, com- pressed air pipes, telegraph and telephone cable ducts Ac. These pipes are surreunded by earth filling, upon which are laid the granolithic pavement slabs. The outer ribs are of a lighter eectio3 than the inner ones, being Mt. by flit. in, at Mt. from the springing and 2ft. Din, by 2ft 3m, at the centre. The outer faces of the outside ribs are indented, forming a plain fillet 9in, wide bevelled into the indented face ; the vertical poets are treated in a similar manner. A deep xi!.

lcading. which cover hundreds of sheets of foolscap; then all the drawings have to be made of every particular, nothing being left to the contlactor, also the specification and bill of quantities. Every little detail is thought out by the engineer. the exact size and position of every poition of the structure being marked on the p`ans. When the woik commences on the site, there is more invisible work, which occasionally gives rise to insulting remarks by ignorant bust bodies, who do not know that the who'e of the work has to be set out onthe site, the details on the drawings being practically transferred full size upon it. To be able td do this important trotk requires many ears study and excerience obtained at a heavy c at. ; but there is practically nothing ,o show tont, outsider for probably a month's hard work. After the work is set out, the foundstiors will be laid. concrete piles being driven to receive the thrust of the arc, es. In the meantime, centres will be con- structed for the main arch ribs and the moulds for the same.

rhe const.uction of the concrete pi'es ill be interesting, steel spirals which ere embedded in the concrete being made on the site by a special machir,e The concrete will be mixed by a patent machine driven by electric power, and then carefuby rammed into the mould. aT, und the steel reinforcing bars, first place in the moulds. The cranes will also be driven by electric power. In a few weeks time the site will be busy from day- light ta dark on both sides of the river with men and machines in active and interesting operation. Abhough reinforced concrete is a splendid material when properly made, many di•-astrous failures have happened on account of either the materials or the workman-hip being bad ; and it is absolutely neces- sary for everything to be the beet of its kind. The Bridges Committee being mindful of this, have very wisely engaged the services of an expert—Mr. Grenville Hodgson—who will inspect.and test every load of material delivered on.the works ; and a second experienced man will see to the exact gauging and proper mixing of the materials, each of wnom anti he under the direct supervision of the resident engineer, Mr. J. Mansfield Cobb. No expense has therefore been spared to secure a good and substantial piece of work, which will an doubt eventually reflect credit upon the Chief Engineer, Mr. Webster, upon the contractors, Messrs. Alfred Thorne and Sons, and all concerned. ARRANGEMENTS FOR THE CEREMONY. . .

The encicsure (On the Cheshite side) wit' be reserved for the use a invited guests. Each pile will be driven by a seam pile (liver, consiwing of a tall structure which will cause an iton monkey " weighing 30cwts. to drop on the head of the pile. ROYAL COURT THEATRE. "The Beggar Princess."

An enthusiastic welccme has been given to Mr. and Mrs. J. Rice Cassidy on their return visit t 3 the Royal Court Theatre this week. Few modern romantic plays have exercised ouch a powerful spell on the playgoer as Mrs. Casaidy's "The Beggar Princess" and its continued popularity is not to be wondered at. The drama is so many-sided in its appeal —em hods, ing as it does, the rude simplicity of peasant and fisher-- life, the glitter and panoply of the Court, arid an exciting under-current of plotting and intrigne —that there is not an uninteresting moment throughout the four acts. A thrilling climax is reached in the fine scene in the banqueting hill of the Palace of Slavonia, when the o'd King is poisoned by the impatient and rebellious heir to the throne and, with his dying words, names as his successor the young Princess who huts few dip before wa, living In obscurity and imagined herself to be a poor daughter of the people. This amazing development creates no little stir amongst the different Court factions and the Princess is at first inclined to forego her claim, but, ferseeing troublous times for the country if the unscrupulous tyrant and poisoner holds the reins of power, she rises to the occasion and quickly gains the confidence of all by her masterly handling of the situation. The dramatic intensity of the olay is relieved by an admirable comedy element for which Mr. J. Rice Cassidy, as the Hon. Algernon Fitzhugh, is chiefly responsible. His delight- ful drollness and quaint Trishisms create many a ripple of merriment, but the character is not solely a humorous one and Mr. Cassidy brings out the m3re serious and touching side with great success. Mrs. Cassidy achieves a notable personal triumph as Seaweed, afterwards the Princess, her portrayal being full of natural charm and gripping the audience by its realism. Mr. Juan D Alberti gives a skillful stay of Duke Sergi us Rubinoff, the of the piece ; Miss Ada Roscoe is an impeiious Countess Olga ; and Miss Kitty John- stone is distinctly amusing as Tommy Boqock, the office boy. The scenery and effects are excellent in every detail. On Wednesday ever irg Mrs. Cassidy's play "The Pinch of Another Man', Shoes " was presented for the firat time in Warrington. It is a powerful work and was very favourably received. The story is based on the complications resulting from this accidental likeness of one man lo another, and there are many striking situati3ns, inrbid:ng a trial scene of absorb- ing interest. Mr. Cassidy was thoroughly at home in the role of Fighting Kit, and Mrs Cassidy won further dist Motif n as Elia An thol.

To-night (S,turday) "The Beggar Princess" will again be btaged NEX f WEEK, " CLAUDIAN."

Next week " Claudian "pays a welceme vis:t to the Royal Court Theatre. The title role trill be taken by Mr. William Maclaren. Ile hae a fihe stage rlresence, n powerful but flexible voice, and thee enable him to pourtray the character in all its atieng,h and impres- .iveness. Mr. Maelaren, who greatly resembles Mr. Wi son Barrett, has p'ayed the part 1,321 times. Miss Lily C. Bandmatin, the talented daughter of Mrs. Panr mina l'almer, ii the leading lady. and she contributes her full share towards the success

of the piece. Both thew artistes are well known 1) local play-goers. The company is a large one, numberivg over Zt'Ct artistes, with full chorus and principal dancer. The etory is a stirring one, and the spectacular effects are excellent, the famous earthquake scene in which Claudian's pa'ace is destroyed being especially realistic and awe-inspiring. "Claudian" was produced in 18M at the Prince's Theatre, London, and has toured the country by Mr. Madmen since the late Mr. Wilson Barrett died is 4. •

THE MUSICAL SOCIETY' OPERA. "SAN TOY"

OPENING OF THE PLAN.

On Wednesday next the plan opens 01 reserved seats at the Warrington Musical Society's performances of the musical comedy "San Toy," and judging by the popularity which this annual event of the society's opera performances has gained it will not be surprising to find that there is a great rush for places. In the cast for "San Toy" there are several names which are pleasur- ably associated with leading parts in past enterprises of the same kind—such people as Mr. J. Rabbitt, Mr. J. W. Bibby. Mr. F. Fieldhouse, Mr. J. C. Bardsley, Mr. J. H. Dr.sper, Mr. W. Moores, and last, but by no means least, Mr.G. H. Smith, will be welcomed in the prominent parts in "San Toy "to which the mere mention of their names naturally allies them. On the other hand the parts for the principal ladies include two new names out of a possible three. It has long been expected that Mrs. C. Garton Stone's vocal and histrionic ability would in the near future be signally recognised, and the fact that the title role of "San Toy" is allotted to her will, we feel confident, ha hailed with pleasure by all patrons of the Musical Society's operatic performances. Miss Sara ,Silcock and Miss Nellie Mullins both have prominent and onerous parts to fill, and there can be little doubt, from what is heard of the rehearsals, that the choice of the Opera Committee will find its full justification when these two ladies come before the footlights. The two acts of "San Toy" present scenes of the busiest animation, occasion being found in the second, at the reception by the Chinese Emperor, for a most graceful and bewitching solo dance which, in the part of " Trixie, " will be undertaken by Miss Marjorie Dodds. There are, however, numerous ensemble and sectional dances and evolutions which can scarcely fail to delight the spectators of them,while the lantern ritual, the solo and choral singing and the scenic displays which go to the keeping of the Full Moon Feast in Act I will, it is confidently antici- pated, enthral both the sight and the hearing of all privileged to be amongst the audience. "San Toy' is just now being most arduously rehearsed, with Mr. F. H. Crossley at his usual post as conductor and musical director, and Mr. R. W. Lomax in the position of dramatic instructor and stage manager, and the public performances are, as will be seen from our advertising columns, fixed to take place on the six nights commencing Monday October 16th in the Royal Court Theatre, in which luxurious place of amusement full justice will be done to the work in regard to scenery, lighting, and other details pertain- ing to its due presentment. _

Below is appended a list of the principal rrsonages in the Musical Society's produc- tion of "San Toy"

I must get me to bed te-night early, Wilt the lark I must rise in the morn, Ere the dew's t if the grass glistn'ng pearly, Ere the reapers are out 'mongst the corn. For to-morrow's the day that will sure be Quite the busiest day of the year, And the tinv'nor must not be before me, For we're starting our Autumn Sale, dear. He's been looking quite black of late, dearie, And I dread lest I'm asked to resign, For he's tired (so am I) of his query " Why can't you get here, sir, by nine," Ah me ! I remember it once Iva, Quite the easiest feat to essay, A feat that the Ship Canal bridge has— Plus the Crossings, made harder each day. I depart at eight-thirty each morning, On the dot (as you'll testify, dear), But I seldom get Lir ere the warning Toot-toot 'of a steamer I hear ; Aye! a toot 'from smile away, nearly, And I sprint for the Bridge with a dash, Nine times out of ten, thcugh, I'm clearly Too late. Gates are closed with a crash! " Do I weep, do I tear my hair ?" dearie, Well I don't, though around me are some One would hardly term Stoics, I fear me. Or they'd hold back the"cuss words" thatcome. Aye, but" cuss words" are vain,they discover, And are not worth a rap—oe'er a one, All my own I keep back till I'm over, And find—that to, car to town's gone. Ah well ! I'm at leant spared the tossing That must needs ft om a tram-ride accrue, And I may catch it up at the Crossing If the five-to nine ' shunter is due. Though it's not worth the fare then, to mount it, For the short ride to Bridge-street above, 11l did, I am sure you'd account it A piece of Extravagance, love ! What a blessing, if each delayed waiter Could inflict (and collect) a small fine. Every time he's detain, d at 'tl2 Equator ' (So-called 'cause of Crossing the Line). If it's true shout Time being Money. What a lot of our cash they must hold ! Wouldn't the Board f director- look funny If we asked 'em to tip up ota gold ! But I must Le in time in the morning. Set ti, alarm for five thirty I pray. That it's hurricane bell may give wart ing, " It is time to be up. and away." Tie your tce to the bedp, nt. old girlie— Eh, what's that ! Oh bother your corn ! What's discomfort so long a, I'm early— Our Antum Sale starts in the mean ! PAGAN.

Preliminary Arrangements

A successful meeting to insuzirrate this year's Baby Show was presided over by the Mayor and Mayoress on Thursday evening. Amongst those present were :—Mr Alderman James I Evans, Councillors Wilson, and Dr McKee, Dr Hutt, Mr J Moore Murray, Mr Walter T Flood, Mr Rowlands Mr A Nield, Miss Brown, Mrs Forshaw. Mrs Hutt, Miss Hughes, Mrs Murray, Miss Pekes, Mrs Ransoms, Miss Waring. He believed in the baby show because of its erlucational value, and he also believed in the prize giving, but at the same time he thought that the people should be taught that there was help at hand which they them- selves could obtain by their own labours if they would only stretch out their hand to do it. The Civic League of Help. The Mothers and Bebies Welcome end other kindred societies, along with the Health Committee were doing a good educa- tional work, but he thought very strongly that the people must be taught how to improve their own con- ditions, and not to expect to have everything provided for them by the generosity of ladies and gentlemen who gave their time and money to the maintenance of the, rions societies. REGIMENTAL COLOURS FOR THE PARISH CHURCH. A Magnificent Record.

A deeply impres,i,e ceremony will take place at the Warrington Parish Church on Friday evening next when the old colours of the 2nd Battalion Prince of Wales' Volunteers (South Lancashire Regiment) will be handed over to the care of the Rector and churchwardens. Few regiments, if any,can boast of such a magnfficent record of service as the 2nd South 'Lancashire Regiment, which was formerly known as the 82nd Foot Regiment. The colours which will be presented on Friday are emblazoned with the names of victories which stand out brilliantly in the pages of English history and it is a matter for great satis- faction that these much-treasured pormessions should find a resting place in the ancient parish church of tbe twith vvbich the regiment has such intimate associations. Each colour bears the tw.) regimental badges—the plume of the Prince of Wales and the Sphinx, superscribed Egypt, the latter bearing reference to the Napoleonic campaigns. The following remarkable roll of battles is inscribed npon the colours : —Louisburg, Martinique, (1762), Havannah. St. Lucia (1778), Monte Video, Roliea, Virniera, Corunna, Talavera, Badajoz, Salamanca, Vittoria. Pyrennees, Nivelle, Orthes, TO•liollhe, Peninsula Niagara. Waterloo, Candabar, (1842), 0 huznee (1842 (°abo° '1812) Marajapore, Sebastopol, Lucknow, New Zealand, South Africa, (189 19021,Relief of Ladysmith The proceeding% on Friday next will be worthy of a memorable occasion. The 4th Battalion South Lancashire Regiment (Territorials) and the Army Service Corps have consented to escort the colon. s to church and the band of the battalion will be in attendance. At seven o'clock * proceaoinn will leave Orford Barracks, consisting of the band of the Bth and 40th Regimental district, agu .rd f honour and ,all the troops stai roped at the Barracks. Proceeding to the Drill Hall, they will then be met by the Territorials, the M.yor of Watrington and civic officials. At 730 the procession wilt be re-formed and march along Sankey-street, Buttermarket-street, and Church-street to the Pariah Chnrch. Torch- bearers sill form an escort on each side of the colours On artival at the Parish Church the procreeionists will be joined by the Church Lads' Brigade. The troops having taken their allotted places in the church, the c lour party will enter and, preceded by the choir and clergy, walk up the centre aisle to ihe chancel step& Here the ceremony of handing over the colours to the Rector will take place. A special form of service will begone through and the proeeed- Mg. will be of a most inspiring and memorable character.

Cap•ain E. Elgood, adjutant cf the Ith S. Lanca- shire Regiment, is issuing the following notice to the members of the local Territorial battalion The Battali in has been invited to take part in this most interesting and impressive spectacle, and the Commanding Officer Lopes that all ranks of: the Battalion will do their utmost to attend, and so continue the spirit of comradeship with the Line and Special Reserve Battalion which exists at present. About 50 N.C.O.'a atd men will be required as Torchbearers to the Colour Party. The Battalion will fall in at the Drill Ball, at 7.15 p.m. on Friday. I:th October 1011, in Review Order.
